--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the Welsh Bards following the reforms of Gruffudd ap Cynan, specifically focusing on the Herald Bards and the various types of musical performers.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>THE WELSH BARDS.</header>

The third class, though listed last, was probably not the least respected. This group consisted of **Herald Bards** <note>original: *Clerwr* or *Arwyddfardd*</note>, who served as the national historians. They were also experts in genealogy, the heraldry of coats of arms, and the works of ancient Bards such as Taliesin and the two Merlins. According to the account given by Giraldus <note>original: *Cambriae Descriptio* (Description of Wales), chapter 3</note> in the following century, they were wonderfully suited for poetry, if imagination is considered one of its main requirements. He claims they could trace the ancestry of their princes and nobles not only back to Rhodri the Great but even to Beli, Sylvius, Aeneas, and eventually to Adam himself. However, their poetry was generally of a more humble variety; it was usually limited to joking, mimicry, insults, and criticism.

Among the musical Bards, the first class was dedicated to performers on the **Harp**. Readers can find more information about them in the following history and in the section on Welsh musical instruments elsewhere in this volume.

The second class included performers on the six-stringed **Crwth** <note>An ancient bowed string instrument</note>; I refer the reader to the same sections for more information about them.

The third class consisted of singers <note>original: "fingers" (OCR error)</note>, whose job was to sing the compositions of the poetical Bards to the accompaniment of others' harps. A variety of other skills was expected of them. "A singer," according to the Laws, "should know how to tune a Harp or Crwth and play several exercises and ornaments: two preludes, a *cwlwm*, a *caniad*, and the 13 principal tunes, including all their flats and sharps <note>original: $^p$</note>. He should also understand the 13 principal styles of expression and how to accent them with his voice to various tunes. He should know the 24 metres of Poetry and the 24 measures of Music, and be capable of composing in two of the *Englyn* metres and one of the *Cywydd* metres. He should read Welsh with propriety and write it accurately, and be skilled in correcting and restoring any old poem or song that has been corrupted by transcribers."

<note>
Technical terms of Welsh music: These terms are very obscure and difficult to translate precisely. Until scholars can fully decode ancient musical manuscripts, consider these definitions:
*Cwlwmn*: A harmonious piece of music with lyrics.
*Colofn*: A "pillar" or fundamental musical part.
*Cydgerad*: Music arranged in parts or harmony.
*Cadair*: A "chair" or masterly piece of music. Performing this likely allowed a Bard to rise to a higher rank or "the chair."
*Caniad*: A tune or song.
*Gofteg*: A prelude or overture.
*Difyr*: A measure or an entertaining melody.
*Mwchwl*: A famous type of music that could only be mastered by a *pencerdd* (Doctor of Music) of the Harp. Note: Three "noble *Mwchwls*" were equal to four *Colofns*; one *Colofn* equaled 10 *cwlwms*; one *Cadair* was equal to 5 *cwlwms*.
</note>

Specimens of all these metres are shown by Dr. Rhys, John Rhydderch, and the Reverend Mr. Gronw Owen (see *Beirdd Môn* by Hugh Jones, octavo, London, 1763), as well as in the records of the *Society of Cymmrodorion*, reprinted in 1778. There are other metres now considered obsolete and irregular, such as the *Triban* or *Englyn Milwr* (The Warrior’s Song), *Englyn o'r hên ganiad* (The Song of the Ancient Strain), *Englyn garhir* (The Song of the Long Thigh), and *Englyn cildwrn* (The Song of the Closed Fist).

> "The Welsh Muse has, at the request of her followers, condescended to wear various other outfits in which she has appeared not only graceful but even with some degree of dignity and ease. Yet the robes she has always taken most pride in are the *Twenty-four celebrated ancient British Metres*, unknown to every other Muse, in which she has always shown unrivalled brilliance."
>
> -> The Reverend Mr. Walters' *Dissertation on the Welsh Language*, p. 51. <-

The 24 Metres of poetry were likely created before the 24 measures of Music, as the latter seem to have been adapted to and based upon them.

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the classification of the Bards to a detailed description of the Eisteddfod, the triennial assembly where their skills were tested and degrees were awarded. It includes the historical statutes governing these contests.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
# HISTORICAL ACCOUNT OF THE WELSH BARDS

At the weddings of the prince or any of the royal blood, the singer <note>original OCR: "finger"</note> attended to the illustrious bride, and at those entertainments was expected to skillfully carve every kind of fowl that might be served to him.

Such, and so varied, were the regular Bards. Through a novitiate and probation of an appointed term of years, and by performing poetical and musical exercises, they earned degrees in the *Eisteddfod* <note>a traditional Welsh festival of literature, music, and performance</note>. As that venerable assembly existed long before the period I am describing, a description of it perhaps should have already been presented; however, I chose to wait until I could display it in its most perfect form, under the patronage of a prince to whom our poetry and music are forever indebted.

The *Eisteddfod* was a triennial assembly of the *Bards*. It was usually held at *Aberffraw* (formerly the royal seat of the princes of North Wales, situated in Anglesey), *Dinefawr* (the royal castle of the princes of South Wales, in Carmarthenshire), or *Mathrafael* (the royal palace of the princes of Powis, in Montgomeryshire). Its purpose was the regulation of poetry and music, the conferring of degrees, and the advancement of rival candidates to the "chair" of the *Eisteddfod* through poetical and musical contests—or confirming the *Chief Bard* who already held that honorable seat.

Wishing to convey a clear idea of this important subject to my readers, I have included an extract, faithfully translated, from the *statute of Prince Gruffudd ap Cynan* concerning the manner of holding an *Eisteddfod*:

> "When the congress has assembled at the appointed place, according to the notice and summons previously issued, they shall choose twelve umpires skilled in the Welsh language, poetry, music, and heraldry. These umpires shall give the *Bards* a subject to sing upon in any of the twenty-four metres <note>the twenty-four codified meters of Welsh specialized poetry</note>, but not in alternating songs <note>original: "amæbean carols"; songs where two singers answer each other</note> or any such frivolous compositions. The umpires shall ensure that the candidates do not descend into satire or personal insults, and shall allow each person sufficient time to compose his *Englyn* or *Cywydd* <note>specific forms of Welsh poetry</note>, or whatever other task they assign. Furthermore, they shall record the names of the various *Bards* present who intend to sing, so that everyone may be called by name in order to the chair to perform his composition. The unsuccessful candidates shall acknowledge in writing that they are overcome and shall deliver their acknowledgment to the Chief Bard (that is, to him who wins the chair). Everyone shall drink to the health of the Chief Bard and pay him fees; he shall govern them until he is overcome in a future *Eisteddfod*."

From this injunction, it appears that the duties which belonged to the "judge of the palace" during the reign of *Hywel Dda* were later held by a commission of umpires.

What served greatly to increase the competition among the *Bards*—if they needed any additional incentive—was the presence of the prince, who usually presided over these contests. The compositions delivered on these occasions are frequently based on historical subjects and are valuable for their authenticity; for it was the business of the *Eisteddfod* not only to set the rules for poetry and music, but to eliminate falsehoods and establish certainty in the reporting of events.

<margin>Notes on the Fourth Song of *Poly-Olbion*.</margin>
> "A custom so good," says Michael Drayton <note>an English poet, 1563–1631</note>, "that had it been judiciously observed, the truth of history would not be so uncertain. For there was, we suppose, a correction of what was faulty in form or matter, or at least a judgment by the hearers upon what was recited. Some have wished for a revival of this practice, so that either the correction of opinions or a change of purpose in publishing might prevent the spread of errors."

Before anyone could be enrolled in the *Eisteddfod*, the permission of the prince or lord within whose jurisdiction he lived was necessary. If he desired to pursue degrees in poetry, he was obliged at his presentation to explain the five *Englyn* metres and sing them in such a way that one of the principal *Bards* would declare, upon his conscience, that the candidate was competent to be admitted. He then became the pupil of one of the principal Bards, whom he was required to attend annually during Lent. Without his teacher's approval, he could make no composition public. For three years (that is, until the next *Eisteddfod*), he remained a non-graduate and was called a *Difgybl Yspas* <margin>John Rhydderch’s *Welsh Grammar*, pages 188, 189.</margin> *cerdd dafod* <note>original: "cerdd dafawd"; literally "art of the tongue," referring to vocal or literary craft</note>, or a probationary student of poetry.

At the next *Eisteddfod*, after three years had expired, the *Difgybl Yspas* was examined for the degree of *Difgybl Disgyblaidd*, or Bachelor of the Art of Poetry. He was required to be well-versed in the five *Englyn* metres, the four *Cywydd* metres, and three *Awdl* metres, and to produce scholarly compositions of his own that were free from the fifteen common errors.

After the same interval, the Bard took the degree of *Difgybl Penceirddiaidd*, or Master of the Art of Poetry, for which he was required to understand the rules of grammar and rhetoric, and to analyze and explain the alliterative...

<summary>This page describes the Eisteddfod, a triennial Welsh assembly for regulating music and poetry, and details the rigorous testing and multi-year progression required for Bards to earn professional degrees.</summary>
<keywords>Eisteddfod, Welsh Bards, Gruffudd ap Cynan, Englyn, Cywydd, Awdl, Bardic degrees, Aberffraw, Dinefawr, Mathrafael, Michael Drayton, poetic meters</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the regulations and curriculum for Welsh Bards and musicians as established by the statutes of Prince Gruffudd ap Cynan. It specifically details the rigorous requirements for obtaining poetic and musical degrees.</meta>

# THE WELSH BARDS

...the alliterative patterns <note>original: "concatenations"</note> of the language; to avoid all errors; and to sing twenty-one of the meters with harmony and in parts.

To the *Pencerd* <note>original: "Pencerd," a Doctor of Poetry or Chief Singer</note>, or Doctor of Poetry, who obtained his degree at the end of the same period, belonged the entire mystery of the art. He knew how to sing in parts and harmony and was well-versed in complex transposed alliteration. Among his qualifications were creativity in choosing poetic subjects, a wealth of knowledge and invention, the authority to make artistic decisions, and a talent for composing praises for the nobility that would be delightful to hear or read and long remembered.

If a *Disgybl* <note>a disciple or student</note> of any degree was caught in taverns or secret places gambling for money with dice or any other game, anyone was authorized to take whatever money was found in his purse. For mockery, insults, or the invention and spreading of lies, the *Disgyblion* <note>the plural of Disgybl</note> were also punished with fines and imprisonment. For, says the law, the Bards shall be well-behaved and peaceful in their manners, friendly in their disposition, and humble in their service to the prince and his followers.

Only those Bards who had earned the degree of *Pencerd* were authorized to teach, and no more than a single pupil was allowed for each *Pencerd*. The pupils were expressly ordered to refrain from mocking their teachers for the "absent-mindedness" or inattention that is natural to a contemplative mind. But the most valued privilege of the *Eisteddfod* <note>a traditional Welsh festival and competition for bards and musicians</note> was the exclusive right to the "chair." All those who aspired to the honor of presiding over the Bards came forward (as the statute prescribes) at the triennial assembly to compete with each other and with the Chief Bard who already held the position. The successful candidate was seated in a magnificent chair and was thereafter called the *Bardd Cadeiriog*, or "Chair-Bard." At the same time, he was presented with a small silver or gold chair, which he wore on his chest as the badge of his office. As his rank was high, his earnings <note>original: "emoluments"</note> were considerable. These came from the *Disgyblion* when they finished using the horsehair-strung harp and were admitted to professional practice; from brides at their weddings; and from the marriage fines of the daughters of all the Bards within his jurisdiction. Additionally, his own daughter received a marriage portion from the prince.

Whoever desired to advance to degrees in music was presented to the *Eisteddfod* by a musical *Pencerd* who vouched for his ability. During his three-year apprenticeship <note>original: "noviciate"</note>, he was called a *Disgybl Yspás hebrádd*, a probationary student of music without a degree. If he learned to play the harp, he was only allowed to use an instrument strung with horsehair—likely so that his clumsy attempts at harmony would not torment the ears of the masters, and to motivate him to study harder in hopes of eventually playing a harp with more resonant and pleasing strings.

His next step was the degree of *Disgybl Yspás graddol*, a graduated probationary student of music. For this, he was required to know ten *cwlums* <note>musical pieces or themes</note>, one *colofn* <note>a major musical composition, literally a "column"</note>, five *cwlums* of *cydgerdd* <note>ensemble or accompanied music</note>, one *cadair* <note>a masterwork piece, literally a "chair"</note>, and eight *caniads* <note>songs or tunes</note>.

He then became a *Disgyblaidd*, or Bachelor of Music, but was first required to master twenty *cwlums*, two *colofns*, ten *cwlums* of *cydgerdd*, two *cadaïrs*, sixteen *caniads*, and the twenty-four measures of music, and to play them with ease and accuracy.

He next became a *Pencerdiaidd*, or Master of Music, a degree which required a preparatory knowledge of thirty *cwlums*, three *colofns*, fifteen *cwlums* of *cydgerdd*, three *cadaïrs*, twenty-four *caniads*, and four *goftlegs* <note>a specific type of complex musical composition</note>, as well as the skill to define them properly and distinctly.

Lastly, he was admitted as a *Pencerd*, or Doctor of Music. He was obliged to know forty *cwlums*, four *colofns*, twenty *cwlums* of *cydgerdd*, four *cadaïrs*, thirty-two *caniads*, and four *goftlegs*. He had to understand all the laws and variations of harmony, especially the twenty-four *Measures of Music*, and explain them as they were written in the book of musical theory. He had to compose a *caniad* judged flawless by the expert Bards and demonstrate all its properties: its divisions, subdivisions, rests, natural notes, flats, sharps, and every change of movement through the various keys. If the *Pencerd* was a harper, he was required to know the three "excellent *Meichiau*" <note>advanced musical pieces or technical standards</note>, which were equal to the four *cadaïrs*, and the three "new *Meichiau*." He had to know and perform all of this in a masterly way, so that the professors would declare him competent to be a composer <note>original: "author"</note> and a teacher of his art.

The *Eisteddfod* was a strict school. A poetic or musical student who, at the end of his three-year term, could not earn a higher degree, was condemned to lose the degree he already held.

We know that before the time of *Gruffudd ap Cynan*, the musical Bards were secondary to the Chief Bard of the Poets.

* This manuscript, called *Llyfr Dosparth* <note>The Book of Classification</note>, no longer exists.

<page-num>16</page-num>

# HISTORICAL ACCOUNT

But I have reasons for thinking that in his reign, and afterwards, they had a chair and a president of their own. In Mr. Pennant's *Tour in Wales*, page 434, there is an engraving of the silver Harp in the possession of Sir Roger Mostyn, "which has been from time immemorial in the gift of his ancestors, to bestow on the *chief of the faculty* <note>The "faculty" here refers to the professional guild of musicians.</note>. This badge of honor is about five or six inches long, and equipped with strings equal to the number of the Muses <note>The nine Muses of Greek mythology; therefore, nine strings.</note>." It was probably worn by the Chief Musician, just as the silver chair was by the chief Poet.

The revenues of the Bards came from presents at princely and other weddings <note>original: "nuptials"</note>, and from fees during their annual circuits at *Christmas, Easter*, and *Whitsuntide* <note>The week of Pentecost.</note>, and in their triennial *clera*, or grand circuit. Their fees and presents were regulated in proportion to their degrees; and the number of visitors was matched to the social status of the person who received them. Likewise, in order to encourage the <term>clerwr</term> <note>A traveling minstrel or circuiting bard.</note> to keep up the language and the memory of the exploits and pedigrees of the Britons, they were allowed a certain sum out of every "plough-land" <note>An old unit of land measurement representing what one yoke of oxen could plow in a year.</note>, and a proportional amount out of every half plough-land in their district. A month before each festival, the pupils asked their teachers what routes they should take in their upcoming circuit, so that too many would not resort to the same part of the country. A <term>Pencerdd</term> <note>A Chief Singer or Master Bard.</note> was not licensed to visit the common people unless he chose to accept a fee beneath his station and dignity; nor could any Bard of an inferior degree appear before the gentry and nobles. The Bards were not permitted to request presents beyond a certain value, under penalty of being deprived of their musical instruments and practice for three years. When this happened, the illegally requested present was forfeited to the prince.

The <term>Eisteddfod</term> was followed by the grand triennial <term>Clera</term>, which was not limited (as the festival circuits were) to <term>commots</term> and <term>cantrefs</term> <note>Medieval Welsh land divisions; a cantref was roughly equivalent to a "hundred."</note>, but extended through all Wales. Such was the benevolence of the Welsh institutions that Bards afflicted with blindness, or any other natural disability, were granted the privilege of <term>Clera</term>, as were the four poetical and five musical graduates. At a wake or festival, a circuiting Bard was not permitted, during its duration, to leave the house he first visited without the consent of the master of the house, or an invitation given to him by another. If he wandered from house to house, or became intoxicated, he was deprived of his <term>Clera</term> fees, which were then given to the church. If he committed any indecency toward a mistress or maid, he was fined and imprisoned, and lost his <term>Clera</term> privileges for seven years.

Every art has its subordinate professors. Besides the four classes of regular or graduated Bards I have described, there were four other classes of inferior and unlicensed Bards (if that name may be given to them without profanation): these were Pipers, Players on the three-stringed <term>Crwth</term> <note>An ancient bowed lyre.</note>, Taborers <note>Players of a small drum called a tabor.</note>, and Buffoons. Regarding the pipe, the three-stringed <term>Crwth</term>, and the tabor, the reader will find some mention near the illustration of the musical instruments of the Welsh. The performers who used them were looked upon among Bards as weeds among flowers; they had no connection with the <term>Eisteddfod</term>, and their reputation and profits were equally insignificant. One of their number, the <term>Datceiniad Pen Pasgwr</term> <note>A vocal performer or declaimer who recited poems without an instrument.</note>, was a minstrel who only rehearsed and played no instrument. On festive occasions, he stood in the middle of the hall where the company was assembled, and beating time with his staff, sang a poem to the band. When any of the regular Bards were present, he attended them as a servant and did not presume to sing unless they signaled their approval.

The only connection that existed between the higher and lower orders of the Bards is found in the appointment of the <term>Cyff Clêr</term> <note>Literally the "butt of the bards" or "stock of the minstrels."</note> at the marriage of a prince, or any person of princely descent. A year and a day before the celebration of the wedding, notice was given to a <term>Pencerdd</term> to prepare himself to take on that role. When the time came, he appeared in the hall, and a humorous subject being proposed, the inferior Bards surrounded him and attacked him with their ridicule. In these mocking, satirical outbursts, they were restrained from any personal insults or real offense. The <term>Cyff Clêr</term> sat in a chair in the middle of them and silently allowed them to say whatever they chose that might serve to entertain the assembly. For this unpleasant service, he received a considerable fee. The next day he appeared again in the hall, answered his revilers, and provoked the laughter and gained the applause of everyone present by exposing them in their turn, throwing all their ridicule back upon them.

At Christmas in the year 1176, <term>Rhys</term>, prince of South Wales, gave a magnificent entertainment with feats of arms and other shows in his new castle of <term>Cardigan</term> or <term>Aberteifi</term>, for a great number of illustrious natives and foreigners. Notice of this had been given a year and a day beforehand by proclamation throughout all Britain and Ireland. The musical Bards of North Wales and South Wales, who had been expressly invited...

<page-num>15</page-num>

# THE WELSH BARDS.

For the festival and a poetical contest, the participants were seated in chairs with much ceremony in the middle of the great hall of the castle. Inspired by their usual competition, the presence of their noble audience, and the expectation of the rich rewards promised to the victors, they continued their noble struggle for a long time. It ended with honor for both parties: the prize for poetry was awarded to the bards of *North Wales*, and the prize for music was given to the domestic musicians of *Prince Rhys* <note>Rhys ap Gruffydd (1132–1197), ruler of the kingdom of Deheubarth, who hosted a famous Eisteddfod at Cardigan Castle in 1176.</note>. In thus entertaining his guests with poetry and music, the Welsh prince (as Lord Lyttelton remarks in his history of *Henry II*) maintained the ancient customs of his country. Furthermore, by the number and skill of the poets and musicians he gathered, he undoubtedly far surpassed what King *Henry* could showcase in the same way to him and the other Welsh chiefs when he entertained them in his royal castle at *Oxford* <sup>w</sup>.

<margin><sup>w</sup> *History of Henry II*, 4to edition, volume III, page 302.</margin>

At this feast, the Bards were confirmed by the prince's authority in the freedoms and privileges granted to them by former statutes. They were also compensated with fees, settled by long-standing custom <note>original: "prescription"</note>, which were proportioned to the rank of their profession and the degree they had obtained within it <sup>x</sup>.

<margin><sup>x</sup> *Powel's History of Wales*, page 205. *Dr. J. D. Rhys's Institutes*, page 296.</margin>

Though the era of *Rhys* was so favorable to the Bards, we would have remained ignorant of the nature of the poetry and music for which they were so highly valued if they had not found a historian worthy of their fame in *Giraldus Cambrensis* <sup>y</sup>. He was a native of the country and traveled through it in search of information with such an industrious and philosophical spirit of learned curiosity—a trait that very rarely occurred in those early times. The manner in which the subject of Welsh music is treated in the following quotation from his *Description of Wales* will sufficiently justify its length.

<margin><sup>y</sup> *Sylvester Giraldus*, or *Giraldus Cambrensis* (Gerald of Wales), from a noble Flemish family near *Tenby* in *Pembrokeshire*, was born in 1145. He was secretary to *Henry II*, tutor to King *John*, and Bishop of *St. David's*. In 1187, he accompanied *Baldwin*, the Archbishop of *Canterbury*, into Wales to preach the Crusade. He wrote an *Irish* and *Welsh Itinerary* among other works. He died and was buried at *St. David's* around the age of 70.</margin>

> "By the sweetness of their musical instruments, they soothe and delight the ear. They are rapid yet delicate in their modulation; and by the astonishing agility of their fingers and their swift transitions from discord to harmony <note>original: "concord"</note>, they produce the most pleasing music. This cannot be better explained than by what I have said in my *Topography of Ireland* concerning the musical instruments of the three nations. It is remarkable that, despite their haste in performance, they never forget timing and musical proportion. Such is their art that, with all their shifts in tone, the variety of their instruments, and the complexity of their harmony, they attain the perfection of consonance and melody through a sweet velocity, an even disparity, and a 'discordant concord.' The strings strike together in intervals of fourths or fifths. They always begin with B-flat and return to it, so that the entire piece is completed with the sweetness of a grand and pleasing sound. They enter into a movement and conclude it in such a delicate manner, playing the small notes so playfully beneath the deeper sound of the bass strings—enlivening the music with light-hearted spirit or communicating a deeper internal sensation of pleasure—that the perfection of their art appears in the very concealment of it. For:
> -> Art profits when concealed, <-
> -> Disgraces when revealed." <note>original Latin: *Ars est celare artem* — "True art is to conceal art."</note>

Here I cannot refrain from interrupting this fascinating narrative by *Giraldus* to introduce some lines from one of *Philips's pastorals* <note>Ambrose Philips (1674–1749), an English poet and contemporary of Alexander Pope.</note>. These lines beautifully describe those effects which the harp is uniquely capable of producing, and for which it is universally admired:

> "Now lightly skimming o'er the strings they pass,
> Like wings that gently brush the plying grass,
> And melting airs arise at their command;
> And now, laborious, with a weighty hand,
> They sink into the chords with solemn pace,
> And give the swelling tones a manly grace."

<margin><sup>z</sup> *Description of Wales* (Cambriae Descriptio), chapter 11.</margin>

> "For this reason, those very strains—which afford deep and unspeakable mental delight to those who have looked closely and skillfully penetrated into the mysteries of the art—fatigue rather than gratify the ears of others. These people, though they see, do not perceive, and though they hear, do not understand. By such listeners, the finest music is esteemed as nothing more than a confused and disorderly noise, and it will be heard with unwillingness and disgust. The Welsh have three kinds of musical instruments: the Harp, the *Crwth* <note>An ancient Welsh bowed string instrument.</note>, and the *Pipes* <sup>z</sup>.

They do not sing in unison like the inhabitants of other countries, but in many different parts. So, in a company of singers, which one frequently meets with in *Wales*, as many different parts and voices are heard,

<summary>This page describes a 12th-century Welsh Eisteddfod hosted by Prince Rhys and provides a detailed historical account by Giraldus Cambrensis regarding the technical sophistication and polyphonic nature of Welsh music.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh musical history, specifically examining the account of polyphonic singing (singing in parts) as recorded by the 12th-century historian Giraldus Cambrensis.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>HISTORICAL ACCOUNT OF THE WELSH BARDS</header>

...heard as there are performers; they all finally unite, with instrumental melody, into a single harmony in the soft sweetness of B-flat.

In the northern parts of Britain, beyond the Humber and on the borders of Yorkshire, the inhabitants use the same kind of symphonic harmony in their singing, but with less variety. They sing only in two parts: one murmuring in the bass, the other warbling in the high or treble range. Neither nation acquired this peculiar skill through formal study, but through long habit, which has made it familiar and natural. This practice is now so firmly rooted in them that it is rare to hear a simple, single melody sung well. Even more wonderful, their children sing in the same manner from infancy."

After this account of the musical traditions of the Welsh, Giraldus's testimony was not even needed to prove that they highly esteemed and practiced music, or that harmony must have existed among them in great perfection. However, from the passages I have quoted concerning their art, we can conclude with a high degree of certainty that they possessed an advancement in music whose invention is usually attributed to <term>Guido d'Arezzo</term> <margin>b</margin> <note>original: "Guido". Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033) was a medieval music theorist whose work developed modern musical notation and the staff.</note>. They were either acquainted with <term>counterpoint</term> <note>Counterpoint: the technique of combining two or more melodic lines to play or sing at the same time.</note> and the method of singing in parts, or Giraldus himself must have invented it and given them the credit for his discovery. I am reluctant to contradict the opinion of so diligent a historian and ingenious a critic as <term>Dr. Charles Burney</term> <margin>c</margin> <note>Dr. Charles Burney (1726–1814) was a famous English music historian who was often skeptical of claims regarding very early polyphony.</note>, but I am persuaded that if he had previously researched the musical studies of the <term>Bards</term> and their public institutions in earlier centuries, he would not have allowed his low opinion of Giraldus's honesty to outweigh the strong evidence provided. If further proof is needed that the Bards understood counterpoint, it can be found in the *Four and Twenty Ancient Games of the Welsh* <margin>d</margin>, where "singing an ode or song in four parts" (*canu cywydd pedwar*) is included. Additionally, the manuscript mentioned on page 12 contains several Welsh tunes in full harmony that can be dated with certainty to the eleventh century, and some even earlier.

---

<margin>a</margin> In the same place, chapter 13. <note>original: "Ibid. ch. 13." referring to Giraldus Cambrensis's *Description of Wales*.</note>

<margin>b</margin> "It is well known that <term>Guido's</term> newly invented counterpoint was expressed in long notes to draw out his harmonious sounds, and his movements were slow. But Giraldus Cambrensis, his contemporary, gives us an amazing account of the speed, execution, and correctness with which the Britons played their intricate and complicated music in parts on their harps. If <term>Guido's</term> invention had only then reached Wales, would they have become experts in its practice so quickly? Or would they have written their music in the crude, old-fashioned style of the manuscript you mention, when a much better method had been discovered? It may therefore be inferred that the Britons performed music harmoniously in parts before the Italians. The characters in the Welsh manuscript were likely chants or recitatives used by musical groups, concerts, and choirs in great houses or during divine worship. We read of the 'Choir of Alun' (*Kor Alun*), 'Choir of Aedan' (*Kor Aedan*), 'Choir of Elvyw' (*Kor Elvyw*), 'Choir of Finwr' (*Kor Finwr*), etc., which signifies a group of voices and instruments joined in harmony."

<summary>This page argues that the ancient Welsh practiced polyphonic harmony (singing in parts) long before it was formally "invented" in Italy, citing 12th-century accounts and the traditional "Twenty-Four Games" of the Welsh as evidence. The text includes a detailed table of these games, which categorize the physical, martial, and intellectual skills required of a Welsh gentleman or Bard.</summary>

<keywords>Welsh Bards, Giraldus Cambrensis, counterpoint, polyphony, Guido d'Arezzo, Dr. Burney, Twenty-Four Games, canu cywydd pedwar, musical history, harp, Bangor, Rev. Mr. Evans</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh musical traditions, transitioning from the technical aspects of part-singing and harmony to the historical role of the Bards as keepers of truth and national record.</meta>

# THE WELSH BARDS.

Even to this day, our self-taught native harpers, who are completely unfamiliar with modern music, retain something of that skill for which the Bards were famous. Like their great predecessors, from whom they have received their tunes through tradition, they perform—however simply—together in groups <note>original: "in concert"</note>. They accompany the voice with arpeggios <note>original: "harpegios"</note>, they delight in musical variations, and without straying from their theme, they indulge in the playful excursions of musical imagination.
Such as he produced them, such let the remains be! <note>original: "Quales fudit eum tales sint reliquiae!" — Phaedrus.</note>

The poetry, as well as the music, of the Bards has been greatly illustrated by the writings of *Giraldus* <note>Giraldus Cambrensis (c. 1146–1223), a chronicler who documented Welsh culture.</note>. He has transmitted to us a memorable example of its adherence to truth and its use in recording events for future generations. In his time, the truthfulness of the Welsh Muse was revealed to the world by an extraordinary discovery. *Henry II* was led to the churchyard of *Glastonbury* in search of the body of *Arthur* by some lines of *Taliesin* <note>Taliesin was a famous 6th-century Welsh poet.</note>. These lines, which described the manner of Arthur's death and the place of his burial, had been repeated in the King's presence by a Welsh Bard. (To borrow from *Michael Drayton*, using one of his beautiful addresses):

> "To *Pembroke* called before the English king,
> And to your powerful harp commanded there to sing,
> Of famous *Arthur* told, and where he was interred,
> In which those reckless <note>original: "wreckeſs"</note> times had long and blindly erred;
> And ignorance had brought the world to such a state
> As now, which scarcely believes that *Arthur* ever was.
> But when King *Henry* sent the reported place to view,
> He found that man of men: and what you said was true."
->*Polyolbion. The Sixth Song.*<-

This is not fiction. The success of the investigation was welcome to the monarch’s poetic faith, and *Henry* had the satisfaction of viewing the magnificent remains and counting the glorious wounds of the last of the *Britons*. <margin>f See also the notes of the third song of *Polyolbion*.</margin> <margin>g *Guthrie’s History of England*, vol. I. p. 102.</margin>

To these incidents Mr. *Warton* <note>Thomas Warton (1728–1790), a prominent literary historian.</note> (with his usual skill and cleverness) has given a new and poetic form in an ode called the *Grave of Arthur*. It possesses so many beauties that I find it difficult to choose a single passage and am discouraged from making a selection.

Regarding the use of our poetry in preserving the memory of events and the aid it has lent to history, the same period produced a similar example. Of the celebrated *Madog ab Owain Gwynedd* and of his discovery of *America* <margin>h For a fair inquiry into this subject, see Lord *Lyttelton’s* notes on the 5th book of his *History of Henry II*. See also *Owen’s British Remains*, 8vo. p. 1. c. 1. Likewise *Carte’s History of England*, p. 638.</margin>, we know nothing but what we gather from the poems of *Cynfrig ap Gronw* and *Meredydd ap Rhys*, and the more explicit declaration of that learned herald and bard, *Guttun Owain*. <note>k *Meredydd ap Rhys* flourished around 1470: *Guttun Owain* in 1480: and *Cynfrig ap Gronw* near the same period.</note> These men all lived before the expedition of *Columbus* and relate or allude to the expedition of *Madog* as a well-known and universally accepted event that had happened three hundred years earlier.

If *Geoffrey of Monmouth*, when he translated *Tysilio* <note>Tysilio was a 7th-century Welsh prince and bishop to whom a Welsh chronicle is attributed.</note>, had known the works of *Taliesin* and *Llywarch Hen*, he might have found in them an abundance of historical passages. These would have served better to enlarge and improve that venerable and authentic history than the legendary tales and unbelievable fictions he adopted.
It is a pleasure to approach the untouched springs. <note>original: "Juvat integros accedere fontes" — Lucretius.</note>

But lest the purity of these genuine sources, which are still unexplored, should be doubted, let it be remembered that the descendants of the *Celts* could never be brought to think like the *Greeks* and *Romans* on the subject of heroic poetry. Heroic poetry was held in such reverence by that primitive nation and its descendants that fable and invention (the essence of the classical epic <note>original: "epopee"</note>) were never allowed to be any part of it. Because of this, neither the *Britons*, the *Irish*, the *Scots* <note>original: "Erse"</note>, the *Cornish*, nor the *Armoricans* <note>The people of Brittany, France.</note> have ever produced a poem similar in its structure to the *Iliad* or *Aeneid*, even though most other nations have shown an inglorious pride in imitating them. What is called an heroic poem and the grandest performance of human art in one country is despised in another as a legendary, empty song. It is seen as calculated to please a vain and boastful people who have no actual deeds of their own virtue and courage to be recorded, but are forced to resort to fictitious...

<summary>This page discusses the historical accuracy of Welsh Bards, noting how their poems led Henry II to King Arthur's grave and recorded Madog's voyage to America before Columbus. It highlights that, unlike classical poets, the Celts prioritized historical truth over fictional invention in their heroic poetry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical accuracy of Welsh Bards, contrasting their authentic chronicles with the fictitious legends of other writers.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>HISTORICAL ACCOUNT OF</header>

fictitious heroes, fictitious battles, and such anachronisms as a serious British writer would have blushed to admit. Historians who are only familiar with works of this sort may understandably view poetry with the contempt they have usually shown—as a vain art that draws its materials more from imagination than nature, and delights in fiction rather than truth. But the Poetry of the British Bards is widely different; from the earliest times, it has been the sacred repository of the deeds of great men.

The period between the reign of *Gruffudd ap Cynan* and that of the last prince, *Llewelyn*, is the brightest in our annals. It abounds with perhaps the most noble monuments of genius and bravery of which the Welsh nation can boast. It is enough for me to mention a few illustrious names who, with a reverence inherited from their great predecessors, received the arts of Poetry and Music and passed them on with even greater honors to their descendants. I wish the limits of this essay allowed me to give more than just their names; or that my learned countrymen would show some of that enterprising spirit for which their ancestors were famous and publish their remaining works for the world to see. <margin>l</margin> The poems of *Meilir*, the Bard of *Gruffudd ap Cynan*; *Cynddelw Brydydd Mawr*; *Owen Gyfeiliog*, prince of *Powys*; *Gwalchmai ap Meilir*; *Gwrgant ap Rhys*; *Llywarch*, the Bard of *Llewelyn* the Great; *Einion ap Gwalchmai*; and *Gruffudd ap yr Ynad Côch* are still in existence and are certainly attributed to their authors. 

But the harmonies of *Albon ap Cynan*, *Rhydderch Foel*, *Cynwrig Bencerdd*, *Cybelyn* <gloss>m</gloss>, and *Cadwgan*—which oblivion has claimed a share of—are only thinly scattered in our manuscripts. The memory of their composers is preserved only by brief mentions in the pages of later poets. Since writing and the practice of music became separate professions, the fame of the poor musician has died away along with the vibration of his strings. The voice of acclaim and the thunder of applause pass away like vapors. Those hands that were most active in showing temporary approval often allow the fame of those who charmed away their cares and sorrows in hours of innocent delight to remain unrecorded. Some of the musical productions of this period are found in the present collection, as well as some that are far more ancient. I decline the task of identifying them with any finality because the original titles are lost. They are now known by other names, substituted by later Bards to compliment later patrons. This observation is specific but necessary; without it, the age of some of the best remains of Welsh Music might be mistakenly identified.

Early in the twelfth century, harmony and verse had reached their highest degree of perfection in Wales. Unlike the common fate of the arts in other countries, they did not suddenly decline from that height. If, in the following age, they showed any signs of decay, a remedy was so diligently applied by the skill of the *Eisteddfod* <note>The Eisteddfod is a traditional Welsh festival of literature, music, and performance, functioning here as a regulatory body for artistic standards.</note> that they preserved their former vigor and perhaps acquired new graces. Had it not been for the fatal catastrophe <note>original: "accident"</note> that overwhelmed the hereditary princedom of Wales in its hour of prosperity, and ruined its poetry and music as well, our country might have kept its ancient government and native arts to this day, protected within the mountains that sheltered them for ages. 

The Poets of these memorable times added energy to a vigorous <note>original: "nervous"</note> language, and the Musicians called forth the harp’s loudest and grandest tones to re-invigorate the ancient struggle of their brave countrymen for freedom and their ancestral soil. The history of the eras in which they flourished best explains the success of their noble purpose. It is a strong proof of their influence that when the brave but unfortunate prince *Llewelyn* the Last <gloss>o</gloss>—after surrendering his rights and sacrificing his patriotism for love <gloss>n</gloss>—was treacherously killed at *Buellt*, Edward I did not feel secure in his triumph until he added cruelty to injustice. He struck the final blow to Welsh liberty with the massacre of the Bards <gloss>p</gloss>. In this shameful deed, *Edward* imitated the policy of *Philip of Macedon*, who demanded the surrender of the *Athenian* orators as a condition of peace. The massacre was general, and as some of our most eminent Bards must have perished, it is probable that many of their works, and the works of those who came before them, were also destroyed and are forever lost. This tragic event inspired one of the noblest lyric compositions in the English language <note>The author is referring to Thomas Gray's famous 1757 ode, "The Bard."</note>: a poem of such fire and beauty that it might, as a recent writer suggested <gloss>q</gloss>, remove our regret over the event and compensate for the loss. But the great merit of this admirable ode actually lies in how it increases our regret; and without—

---

<margin>l</margin> The names and dates of these Bards can be found in the catalog of British authors published by Dr. *Davies* and Mr. *Richard* in their Dictionaries of the Welsh Language. Some extracts from their writings are included in Mr. *Evans*’s specimens of Welsh Poetry and his *Dissertation on the Bards*. There is also an extensive catalog of the Bards' works in Mr. *Llwyd*’s *Archæologia Britannica*, page 254, etc.

<margin>m</margin> Sister of Cybelyn, fair little man / A flute, six times swifter. <note>original Welsh: "Chwaer Cybelyn befrddyn bach, / Chiwbanogl, chwe’ buanach."</note> — *Dafydd ap Gwilym*.

<margin>n</margin> Dr. *Burney*’s History of Music, volume II, page 70.  
<margin>o</margin> See *Wynne*’s History of Wales, 1774 edition, page 283.  
<margin>p</margin> See *Guthrie*’s historical Grammar.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh poetry's historical value, moving from the medieval era to the period of English conquest and the subsequent 15th-century uprising under Owain Glyndwr.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>THE WELSH BARDS.</header>

<margin><sup>r</sup> Michael Drayton, through information from his friend Mr. John Williams, was extremely well-informed about the Bards and their institutions. His accurate knowledge is expressed in the *Polyolbion* through elegant and spirited poetry.</margin>
Without giving it extravagant praise, I can boldly say that the *Polyolbion* by Drayton<sup>r</sup> and *The Bard* by Gray have boosted their authors' reputations as much as they have the glory of Wales. These are the only modern works worthy of making up for the massive loss of literary treasures and the irreparable destruction of Welsh genius.

After the end of the princely government in Wales, the English exercised such tyranny over the conquered nation that the Bards who were born "since Cambria's fatal day" <note>This is a reference to the legendary massacre of the bards by Edward I, a popular theme in 18th-century literature</note> seemed to rise under a cursed and malignant star. They were forced to practice their sacred art in obscurity and sorrow, compelled to suppress the anger that would otherwise have burst out in the most animated songs against their ungenerous and cruel oppressors. Yet they were not idle. So that their poetry could safely express their patriotism, it became dark, prophetic, and oracular. Just as the monks of the Welsh church had written several religious poems and attributed them to <term>Taliesin</term> <note>Taliesin was a famous 6th-century primary bard; later writers often used his name to give their own work more authority</note> to support their own doctrines in their controversy with Rome, the Bards now credited many of their political works to that same venerable author, or produced others as the prophecies of the elder Merlin. This has led to great uncertainty regarding which 6th-century remains are genuine, as a great many of them have reached us damaged or altered. This also explains the mysterious atmosphere that pervades all the poetry of the later periods I am currently describing. The forgery of those poems, even if they were accepted as real by critics such as Dr. Davies and Dr. J. D. Rhys, can be easily detected today. They were written for popular, temporary purposes and were not crafted carefully enough to hide their historical errors, modern language, and other marks of being fake from a knowledgeable scholar.

<margin><sup>s</sup> *Dissertation on the Bards*, p. 92. <note>original: "Dissertatio de Bardis"</note></margin>
While the Bards were restricted in their poetic freedom, they had more time to study heraldry and perform their other domestic duties. Every nobleman supported an eminent Bard who, upon the lord's death, composed a funeral poem about his lineage, honors, and life story. This poem was then solemnly recited by a <term>Datceiniad</term> <note>original: "Datceiniad"; a professional singer or reciter who performed a Bard's compositions</note> in front of the surviving family<sup>s</sup>. This is why genealogies are so well-preserved in Wales.

<margin>
<sup>t</sup> *Owen's Memoirs of Owain Glyndwr*, 4to. London 1775, and *Pennant's Tour in Wales*, p. 302, etc. The generosity and exploits of this daring chief are celebrated in the most animated songs by that famous and learned Bard, Iolo Goch.
<sup>u</sup> *Evans's Specimens of Welsh Poetry*, p. 160. *Pennant's Tour in Wales*, p. 325, 330.
</margin>
However, the uprising during the reign of Henry IV revived the martial spirit of the <term>Awen</term> <note>original: "Awen"; the Welsh word for the Muse or poetic inspiration</note> to celebrate the heroic enterprises of the brave Glyndwr. Like him, the Bards of that era were "irregular and wild." Just as a candle flickering in its socket gives one sudden bright flash before it goes out, they made one last brilliant effort of their original and daring genius, which was then lost and buried forever in the grave with their hero. Yet though poetry flourished, learning suffered; for the reckless fury of that famous partisan and his enemies against the monasteries was so great that not only the monks' cells but also their libraries and manuscripts were destroyed<sup>u</sup>.

The following *Ode to Glyndwr* by his favorite Bard, Gruffudd Llwyd—beautifully translated into English verse by Mr. Williams of Vron—deserves a distinguished place in this history because of the author's genius and the translator's skill.

---

### O D E.

<margin>
<sup>w</sup> *Pennant's Tour*, p. 311.
<sup>z</sup> Owain Glyndwr, descended from the ancient race of British princes, first appeared in arms against Henry IV in the year 1400. He attacked the lands of his enemy, Lord Grey, and immediately recovered what had been unjustly taken from him; soon after, he had himself proclaimed Prince of Wales. His chief Bard, Gruffudd Llwyd, regretting his absence, chants his praise and predicts the success of the war in a <term>Cywydd</term> <note>original: "Cywydd"; a traditional Welsh poetic form with strict rules of rhyme and alliteration</note>. This *Cywydd*, or Ode, is elegantly translated from the Welsh by the Rev. Mr. Williams of Vron.
</margin>

<meta>This page provides a poetic account of the Welsh leader Owain Glyndŵr, drawing on the works of his contemporary bard, Gruffudd Llwyd. It parallels Glyndŵr’s military exploits with the legendary prophecies of Merlin and the lineage of Uther Pendragon.</meta>

<note>This first section is a modernization of the original Welsh poem by Gruffudd Llwyd, addressed to Owain Glyndŵr.</note>

One night, speaking aloud,
I was at a feast with plenty of mead,
My treasure <note>original: "fy ngrair"</note>, I often joked
In your court, where I received wine from your hand.
Mead encouraged more sacred customs,
But my behavior was worse for my upbringing. <note>This suggests the bard’s boldness or drunken behavior was forgiven by his patron.</note>

->2.<-

Lord of great praise with the strength of nine soldiers,
No man could refuse you.
In the hour you went in anger
To Britain <note>In this context, *Prydain* refers to the English territories or the crown's lands</note> to prepare destruction,
The captive grief of longing was close
To bringing me to death because of you!
The memory of you did not leave me,
Golden spear, for an hour without weeping!

->3.<-

Tears start across my cheek,
Pouring like rain that soaks it;
When my struggle was heaviest
For you, son of a generous father,
I heard from the lips of a messenger—
May you have God’s grace and a true status!—
That you were found in the battle, generous heart,
Gaining great fame, my Lord!

<margin><sup>y</sup> The omen referred to was a star and fiery dragon; which, according to the interpretation of *Merlin*, predicted the reign of *Uther*, who was afterwards surnamed *Pendragon* (Head-Dragon). This was because he had two golden dragons made: one he presented to the cathedral of *Winchester*, and the other he carried with him in his wars, or, what is more likely, wore as a crest on his helmet. His son *Arthur* adopted the same. See *Geoffrey* of Monmouth, pages 254, 257, 283.</margin>

->4.<-

It was prophesied in a time of heavy tumult;
God and man, if you were to come there;
My soul, above the stony banks of the River Dee <note>original: "DYFRDWY FAENAWR"</note>,
My Lord cast many to the ground.
If I, as a prophet, speak it
In this place, guiding the song:
You found good fortune in your venture,
Like **Uther Pendragon**, with his breastplate of steel:
When he avenged the insult suffered
By his brother, and his power, and his battle.

<note>The following is an 18th-century English poetic paraphrase of the Welsh verses above.</note>

**River Dee** <note>original: "DWRDWY"</note>, whose wide-spreading streams,
Reflecting the moon’s <note>original: "Cynthia’s"</note> midnight beams,
Once <note>original: "Whilom"</note> led me to your bower;
Alas! In an unguarded hour!
For high in blood, warmed by Welsh mead <note>original: "Britiſh beverage hot"</note>,
My respectful distance I forgot;
But soon my generous chief overcame
The rude presumption of his slave.

->2.<-

But do not leave me, illustrious lord!
Your peaceful bower and hospitable board
Are poorly exchanged for scenes of war,
Though **Henry** <note>King Henry IV of England</note> calls you from afar.
My prayers and my tears were in vain;
He flew like lightning to the hostile plain.
While with remorse, regret, and woe,
I saw the god-like hero go;
I saw, with an aching heart,
The golden beam depart.
His glorious image in my mind
Was all that **Owain** left behind.
Wild with despair and overwhelmed with grief,
Your faithful Bard is left alone,
To sigh, to weep, to groan!

->3.<-

Your sweet remembrance, ever dear,
Your name, always introduced by a tear,
Speaks of my inward anguish;
How could you, cruel **Owain**, go,
And leave the bitter streams to flow
Down **Gruffudd’s** furrowed cheek?
I heard (who has not heard of your fame?)
With ecstasy I heard your name,
Loudly echoed by the trumpet of war,
Which declared you brave and void of fear;
Yet possessed of a gentle heart,
That bled within your generous breast,
To see, across the bloody <note>original: "ſanguine"</note> plain,
The havoc of hostility.

->4.<-

May you still fight with good omens,
And do right by your injured country!
Like the great **Pendragon**<sup>y</sup> shall you soar,
Who commanded the din of battle to roar,
In the time he drew his vengeful steel
To renew his brother’s grandeur,
And justify his wrongs;
His gallant actions are still told
By youthful Bards and by old **Druids**,
And in the songs of grateful Wales <note>original: "Cambria"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the poetic praise of the Welsh leader Owain Glyndŵr, comparing his military prowess to the legendary knight Owain son of Urien. ### THE WELSH BARDS.

->5.<-

*You steered and journeyed your course,* <note>original Welsh: "Llywiaist siwrneaist belynt..."</note>
*Like fine Owain son of Urien of old,* <note>Owain son of Urien was a 6th-century prince of Rheged and a famous hero in Welsh tradition and Arthurian legend.</note>
*When he was a swift combatant—*
*The black knight of the water.*
*He struck with steel in battle*
*Against a dragon’s head with his spear yonder;*
*They were men who brought success to the army,*
*With mighty strength to shatter spears.*
*You also, Owain, on your journey through the clouds,*
*Earnestly made a path of blood with your blade.*
*With your red spear bringing grief to a hundred,*
*And your fair name, and your great power.*

->5.<-

On sea, on land, you still did brave
The dangerous cliff and rapid wave;
Like Urien, who subdued the knight;
And put the deadly dragon to flight,
    By that moss-grown fountain;
The grim, black warrior of the flood,
The Dragon, gorged with human blood;
    The water's scaly pride,
Before his sword the mighty fled:
But now he is numbered with the dead.
Oh! may his great example fire
My noble patron to aspire
To deeds like his! Impetuously fly,
And bid the Saxon <note>The English</note> squadrons die:
So shall your laureled bard rehearse
Your praise in never-dying verse;
Shall sing the prowess of your sword,
Beloved and victorious Lord.

---

->6.<-

*A brother in deed to your word,* <note>original Welsh: "Brawd unweithred i'th eir..."</note>
*A favored judgment, like the son of tall Urien.*
*Everyone yonder saw from your clean hand*
*A worthy journey of praise, a spear of gripping fire.*
*When your labor was heaviest,*
*Yonder, struggling on a wall,*
*Your ash-spear broke with you—*
*A gentle treasure, earnest in the battle-shout:*
*A brave staff, steel was its head,*
*Shattered across a stone into three splinters.*

->6.<-

In future times your honored name
Shall emulate brave Urien's fame!
Surrounded by the numerous foe,
Well did you deal the unequal blow.
How terrible your ash-wood spear,
Which shook the bravest heart with fear
    Beneath those hostile towers!
More horrid than the lightning's glance,
Flashed the red meteors from your lance,
    The harbinger of death.
Dire, and more dire, the conflict grew;
Thousands before your presence flew;
While borne in your triumphal car,
Majestic as the god of war,
Amidst charging hosts unmoved you stood,
Or waded through a sea of blood.

---

->7.<-

*Until Judgment Day, says your poet,* <note>original Welsh: "Hyd ddydd brawd medd dy wawdydd..."</note>
*Born of proud ones, you will be notable.*
*Your holy, two-edged, bright blade*
*Hunts for battle; you sail well to Britain.*
*While breaking a path in your white robe,*
*With your rush to the field and your spears.*
*You caused, my lord, by your labor,*
*Bolts of lightning between the shield-slats and the steel.*

->7.<-

Immortal fame shall be your reward <note>original: "meed"</note>
Due to every glorious deed;
Which the latest annals shall record,
Beloved and victorious Lord!
Grace, Wisdom, Valor, all are yours,
Owain Glyndŵr <note>original: "OWAIN GLYNDWRDWY"</note> divine!
A fitting emblem of a two-edged sword,
Dreaded in war, in peace adored!
Steer your swift ships to Albion's <note>Britain's</note> coast,
Full of your martial host.
Your robes are white as driven snow,
And Virtue smiles upon your brow:
<note>The following three lines are grouped with a bracket in the original margin to indicate a triplet rhyme.</note>
But terrible in war you are,
And swift and certain is the dart
You hurl at a Saxon's heart.

<header>HISTORICAL ACCOUNT OF THE WELSH BARDS</header>

### 8.

Although heroic poetry was afterward no longer attempted in Wales, a long series of Bards followed who, through their elegies and odes, have made their names memorable for ages. Among these, *Dafydd ap Gwilym*$^z$, the "Welsh Ovid," holds a well-deserved preeminence. He often adds the sublime to the beautiful, of which his *Cywydd y Daran*$^a$, or "Ode of the Thunder," is a noble proof. It is a description of a well-chosen scene, admirably varied: it opens with peaceful ideas and rural images—a lovely maiden and a delightful view—then follows a sudden and tremendous change of the elements; the beauties of nature are overshadowed and concealed; the terror of animals, and the shrieks of the fair lady. A thousand instances of similar excellence could be produced from the writings of this elegant Bard and his contemporaries. Let those who complain that, due to the current scarcity of works of genius, they are reduced to reading *Horace*, *Pindar*, and *Gray* for the tenth time, explore the buried treasures of Welsh poetry; their search will be rewarded with new sources of pleasure and new beauties of language and imagination.

The accession of a <term>Tudor</term> to the throne was the happy era destined to recall the exiled arts of Wales, and <term>Henry VII</term> was destined to be the patron and restorer of the Cambro-British Muses. Although the <term>Eisteddfods</term> <note>A traditional Welsh festival of competition in music and poetry.</note> had been discontinued during the previous unfavorable reigns, they were now re-established. The Bards were employed in the honorable task of determining the king's pedigree from their authentic records$^b$. Even <term>Henry VIII</term>, the stern and cruel son of a mild father, did not refuse the Bards his smiles and favor$^c$. As an example, I include the following summons to an <term>Eisteddfod</term> issued by his authority:

"Let it be known to all people, both the gentry and the commoners, that an <term>Eisteddfod</term> of the professors of Poetry and Music will be held in the town of <term>Caerwys</term>, in the county of <term>Flint</term>, on the 2nd day of July, 1523, and the—"

---

$^z$ He flourished around the year 1400. See the titles of some of his poems in the catalogue of British Manuscripts in Mr. *Edward Lhuyd’s Archaeologia Britannica* <note>Published in 1707, this was the first major work of comparative Celtic linguistics.</note>.
$^a$ See his poem published by Mr. *Rice Jones* in *Gorchestion Beirdd Cymru* <note>The Masterpieces of the Poets of Wales (1773).</note>. For the following remarks, I am grateful to that excellent Welsh critic, the late Mr. *Lewis Morris*. "Mr. *Pope*, in his Preface to the Iliad, when listing Homer’s excellencies, places his imitative sounds <note>Onomatopoeia; words that sound like what they describe.</note> next to his boundless invention, and claims they are unique to him and *Virgil*, saying no other poet ever reached this level of art.
"*Dafydd ap Gwilym*, if I am not mistaken, also has a strong claim to this excellence. You must either believe in the philosophy of atoms, or that by copying nature through its own light, he intended his *Ode of the Thunder* to sound like what it really is—a description of thunder and lightning. Yet in his love poems and other soft subjects (of which I currently have nearly a hundred), he is as smooth and glides as easily as an Italian song. Let those who are not overly biased toward the classical languages and are proper judges of our own language compare this poem—its sounds and the loftiness of its metaphors—with the best passages of this kind in the authors mentioned above. I do not doubt they will find this bold comparison excusable, no matter how sacred Homer's character may be." *Down the old ages.* <note>original Welsh: Thylaw’r hen oesodd.</note>
$^b$ *Wynne’s History of Wales*, p. 325, 1774 edition.
$^c$ See Mr. *Evans’s* address *To the Welsh*; Specimens of Welsh Poetry, p. 157.

<summary>This page concludes a poetic tribute to the Welsh leader Owain Glyndŵr and discusses the subsequent history of Welsh poetry, specifically highlighting the genius of Dafydd ap Gwilym and the revival of the Eisteddfod under the Tudor kings Henry VII and Henry VIII.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Welsh Bards, specifically focusing on the formal regulation of the bardic order under the Tudor monarchs Henry VIII and Elizabeth I, and the preservation of their poetic competitions.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>THE WELSH BARDS.</header>

"In the 15th year of the reign of *Henry VIII*, King of England, under the commission of the said king, before *Richard ap Howel ap Ivan Vaughan, Esq.*, by the consent of *Sir William Griffith* and *Sir Roger Salisbury*, and the advice of *Griffith ap Ivan ap Llywelyn Vaughan*, and the Chair-Bard, *Tudor Aled*, and several other gentlemen and scholars, for the purpose of instituting order and government among the professors of *Poetry* and *Music*, and regulating their art and profession <gloss>d</gloss>."

After a long interval of anarchy among the Bards, commissioners were appointed by *Queen Elizabeth* to assemble another <term>Eisteddfod</term> <note>A traditional Welsh festival and competition of music and poetry.</note> at *Caerwys* in 1568 <gloss>e</gloss>. They were instructed to advance the ingenious and skillful to the accustomed degrees, and restore to the graduates their ancient exclusive privilege of exercising their profession. "The rest not worthy" were by this commission commanded to betake themselves to some honest labor and livelihood, on pain of being apprehended and punished as vagabonds <gloss>f</gloss>.

In a private collection of manuscripts, I fortunately met with the following beautiful extempore stanzas on the Nightingale, which were the fruit of the *poetical contest of the Bards of North Wales and South Wales* for the chair, in a later *Eisteddfod at Caerwys* <gloss>g</gloss> in the same reign. They are a curious relic; they show the poetry of our country in its utmost extent of alliterative and musical refinement; and are the only specimen of the kind that has ever been displayed from the press.

### STANZAS TO THE NIGHTINGALE <gloss>h</gloss> <note>original: ENGLYNION I’R EOS</note>

->From the work of various Poets of North Wales and the South, at the Eisteddfod in the Town of Caerwys.<-

| | |
|---|---|
| I heard a fair, golden voice after lurking through the night—a gentle nightingale from the grove; staying for a maiden, on the hem of a fresh, mild field—a nightingale with a lovely voice beneath a leafy bush! <note>original: Clywais dêg eurllais wedi gorllwyn - - nôs, / I 'maros a morwyn : / Ar lawes maes irlaes mwyn, / Eos glwyflais is glafllwyn !</note> | I read of the gentle nightingale of the grove in that place; I have often wondered how one so small, with such a cruel, clear voice, can produce such a sound from the heart of a thicket! <note>original: Eos fwyn o'r llwyn darlleniais - - y mann / Mynych i rhyfeddais ; / Lleied hon greulon groywlais / Mewn torr llwyn a maint yw'r llais !</note> |
| I am healthy and happy since I heard, upon the birch, the arrival of that sweet voice; I recognize the voice of the grey-winged bird—the grey-backed nightingale with the light song! <note>original: Jâch lawen ydwyf o chlywais - - ar fedw, / Arfodi pereiddlais ; / Edyn llwyd adwaen 'i llais, / Eos gefnllwyd yſgafnllais !</note> | Despite the voices of the many small birds I loved, and their strange, gentle songs; the nightingale with the beautiful beak beneath the thorn-bush is the wine-toned organ of the hedge! <note>original: Er llais tra hoffais trafferth - - mân adar, / A'u mwyn wawdydd dierth ; / Eos drwynbert is draenberth / Yw'r gwin bwnge organ y berth !</note> |
| Music from the forest's edge within the heart of the grove—a joy until Judgment Day; the slender, beautiful, humble nightingale is among the branches, weaving a fine tapestry of praise! <note>original: Miwfig min coedwig mewn ceudawd - - y llwyn, / Llawenydd hyd ddyddbrawd ; / Mae'r Eos feindlos fwyndlawd / Mewn y gwŷdd yn mân wau gwawd !</note> | There is no trill or shake harsher than the organ, nor any fair song more true; there are no finer or sweeter notes—there is no sport like the little nightingale! <note>original: Nid cwafriad crychiad crochach - - no'r organ, / Neu gowirgerdd degach, / Nid manwl nodau mwynach / Nid yſbort ond Eos bach !</note> |
| The bright, gentle whistle of the sacrificial bell of the grove is a beautiful joy; music without effort among the thorns—sweet is her lovely sound in the leafy bush! <note>original: Mwynlan gloyw chwiban cloch aberth - - y llwyn, / Mae'n llawenydd prydferth : / Miwſig heb poen ymmyſg perth / Mwyn ei glwyſownge mewn glafberth !</note> | The learned, moral music of the Nightingale's song is of the degree of the spiritual Muse; a gentle descant of intense knots of praise—a descant to natural learning! <note>original: Dyfgedig fiwfig foefawl - - gerdd Eos, / Gradd Awen yſbrydawl, / Deſgant mwyn dwys gnottie mawl / Deſgant i'r dyſg naturiawl !</note> |
| A measured carol under the walls of the leafy bush—a tickling song of joy; sweet music among the small trees—the nightingale through the night under the branches! <note>original: Meſurol garol dan geurydd - - glafberth, / Gogleifbwnge llawenydd, / Miwſig mwyn ymmyſg manwydd / Eos hyd y nôs dan wŷdd !</note> | I heard from a green park and port, before the break of day—it was no foolish song; while the harmonious notes echoed together, I loved the song of the beautiful nightingale! <note>original: Clywais o barc glas a bort, / Cyn nod dydd nid caniad hurt, / Cyd eilio 'ibonge cydlais bart, / Cerais bwnge yr Eos bert !</note> |

->**Sion Tudur.**<-

For the commissioners mentioned here, having in 1568 constituted *Simmwnt Vychan* Chief Bard, appointed another *Eisteddfod* to be held in 1569, the tenth year of Queen Elizabeth's reign. It was chosen for this purpose, in compliance with the ancient custom of the Welsh, because it had been the princely residence of *Llywelyn* the Last. See *Pennant's Tour*, p. 427.

The *Eisteddfod* which produced them was held, I conclude, between the year 1569 and 1580, as the Bards who composed them flourished before or at this latter period. Some of the competing Bards took degrees in the *Eisteddfod* in 1568: *William Llyn* was admitted to the degree of <term>Pencerdd</term> <note>Chief Bard or Doctor of Song.</note>, and *Sion Tudur*, *William Cynwal*, and *Huw Llyn* began as <term>Disgyblion Penceirddiaid</term> <note>Masters-in-training or candidates for the rank of Chief Bard.</note>.

Toward the end of Queen Elizabeth’s reign, flourished **Twm Bach** (or Thomas Pritchard), who was the "Orpheus on the Harp" of that time. He was born at Coity in Wales, died in London in 1597, and was buried in St. Sepulchre’s Church. We can be certain that Poetry shared the grief of its sister art, Music, over this loss by the following two-part **Englyn** <term>Englyn</term> <note>A traditional Welsh short poetic form, often characterized by strict alliteration and rhyme.</note> written upon his death; the first two lines were by Hugh Griffith, and the remainder by **Rhys Cain**.

> Farewell to Little Tom; he has gone to the grave; now...
> <note>original Welsh: *Yn iach i Dwm Bach ; aeth i’r bedd ; - bellach*</note>
> Ah, see! our last, best lyrist goes:
> Harmony <term>Cynghanedd</term> <note>The complex system of alliteration and internal rhyme in Welsh poetry.</note> has failed:
> <note>original Welsh: *E’ ballodd Cynghanedd :*</note>
> Sweet as his strain be his repose!
> I know of no one in his wake, in any way...
> <note>original Welsh: *Ni wn i’w ôl, yn un wedd,*</note>
> Extinct are all the tuneful fires,
> Who knows music on the fingers. <gloss>k</gloss>
> <note>original Welsh: *A wyr fiwfig ar fyfedd*</note>
> And Music with **Twm Bach** expires:
> No finger now remains to bring
> The tone of rapture from the string.

<note>i Richard Davis, Doctor of Divinity, Bishop of St. David’s; he was one of the translators of the New Testament into Welsh (published in quarto, London, 1567). See *An Historical Account of the Welsh Translations of the Bible* by Thomas Llewelyn, LL.D. (octavo, London, 1768).</note>

<note>k From a manuscript of *Englynion* in the library of Jesus College, Oxford.</note>

We see that the *Eisteddfod* was still held in high regard, as even bishops did not disdain to be counted among the ranks of the Bards.
