<keywords>Nightingale, Eos, Twm Bach, Thomas Pritchard, Englyn, Eisteddfod, harp, Welsh poetry, William Cynwal, William Llyn, Huw Llyn, Richard Davis, Bishop of St. David's, Coity, St. Sepulchre's Church, Cynghanedd</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page transitions from specific poetic examples of Welsh bards to a broader historical and linguistic analysis of Welsh music and poetry, beginning with the influence of the harper Powel on the composer Handel.</meta>

# THE WELSH BARDS.

In the reign of George II, Powel, a Welsh Harper who used to play before that *Monarch*, drew such tones from his instrument that the great Handel was delighted with his performance. Handel composed for him several pieces of music, some of which are in the first set of *Handel’s Concertos*. He also introduced him as a performer in his oratorios, in which there are some songs with *Harp Obligato* <note>A parts of a musical score that must be played exactly as written, often as a solo accompaniment</note> that were accompanied by Powel: such as, “*Tune your Harps*” and “*Praise the Lord with cheerful voice*” in *Esther*, and “*Hark! he strikes the golden lyre*” in *Alexander Balus*.

Having now conducted the short history I intended nearly to our own times, I will pause briefly before bringing it to its conclusion to examine more minutely the causes that conferred such peculiarity and excellence on the poetry and music of Wales. The laws, manners, and fortunes of nations have a principal influence in giving an original character to national arts. The first care of the Welsh laws was the freedom of the people. They were free, and their manners accordingly were at once generous and impetuous; gentle, hospitable, and social among their friends, and full of resentment and revenge against their enemies. They inhabited a country where they found in the works of nature what they afterwards copied into their own: the beautiful and the sublime. They were equally addicted to love and war. When they forsook the camp, they did not return to agriculture, commerce, or the mechanical arts, but passed their leisure in hunting and other manly sports and games, in conversation with women <margin>Lord Lyttelton, from *Giraldus Cambrensis, History of Henry II*, vol. II. p. 69.</margin>, and in recounting their exploits amidst drinks of mead at the tables of lords and princes. From this, they learned to write verse and play the harp.

“Another cause, which operated with equal power on our poetry, was the strength and beauty of the language in which it was conveyed; or it may be said with greater truth that those inherent properties of the language were called forth by the poetry itself. The character of Welsh poetry, and its dependence on the language, have been so well displayed in a dissertation on the subject by the Reverend Mr. Walters <margin>A Dissertation on the Welsh Language. 8vo. Cowbridge. 1771.</margin> that I am unwilling to use his sentiments in any other words than his own.

The Welsh language, he observes, possesses native ornaments and unborrowed treasures. It rivals the celebrated Greek in its ability to form the most beautiful derivatives, as well as in the elegance, facility, and expressiveness of an infinite variety of compound words. It deserves the praise given to it even by an enemy <margin>The author of the *Letters from Snowdon*.</margin> that ‘*notwithstanding the many gutturals and consonants with which it abounds, it has the softness and harmony of Italian, with the majesty and expression of Greek.*’”

*I have not experienced, under the heavens,*
*A web as strict as the fair Welsh language.* <note>original: "Ni phrofais, dan ffurfafen, / Gwe mor gaeth a'r Gymraeg wen." Written by Edmund Prys, D.D., Archdeacon of Merioneth.</note>

<column-break/>

Of all the tissues ever wrought
On the Parnassian <note>Parnassus is a mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses and poetry</note> hill,
Fair Cambria's <note>Wales</note> web, in art and thought,
Displays the greatest skill.

---

> “The glory of a language is a full-sounding richness, a vigorous tone, and a clear and expressive brevity, of which a thousand happy instances might be produced from the Cambro-British <note>Welsh</note> manuscripts. Their range reaches from the sublimity of the ode to the conciseness of the epigram. Whoever explores these ancient and genuine treasures will find in them the most melodious rhythms, the most poetical diction, the most powerful expression, and the most elevated sentiments to be found in any language.”

A language, however fortunate in its original construction, can never attain such perfection without a very high degree of cultivation <margin>Dr. Llewelyn ingeniously refers the curious and delicate structure of the Welsh language to its peculiar property of varying its initial consonants artificially, for the sake of harmony <note>original: "euphoniæ gratiâ"</note>; making it superior in this respect to Hebrew and Greek. See *Historical and Critical Remarks on the British Tongue*, 8vo. London, 1769. p. 58, etc. Likewise *Ancient British Language* <note>original: "Antiquæ Linguæ Britannicæ"</note>, by Dr. Davies, 8vo. London, 1621.</margin>. It is evident, therefore, that at some remote period the Welsh themselves were highly cultivated and had made great progress in learning, arts, and manners, since we discover such elegance, design, and philosophy in their language. Some authors have attributed this refinement of the Welsh dialect to the Druids. I disagree with this opinion because I observe that Taliesin <note>A famous 6th-century Welsh bard</note> and his contemporaries, who were followed and imitated by others, do not provide such examples of polished rhythm and diction as the Bards who lived under the later princes. The *Eisteddfod* <note>A traditional Welsh festival of literature, music, and performance</note> was the school in which the Welsh language was gradually improved and brought at last to its unrivalled perfection. “The Bards,” says the ingenious critic I quoted before, “have always been considered by the Welsh as the guardians of their language and the preservers of its purity.”

The metre of Welsh poetry is very complex and alliterative, possessing such peculiar ingenuity in the selection and arrangement of words as to produce a rhythmic chain of sounds in every verse. To...

<column-break/>

<summary>This page discusses the historical influence of Welsh harpers like Powel on Handel and explores why Welsh poetry and language are uniquely sophisticated, crediting the Eisteddfod and the Bards rather than the Druids for its refinement.</summary>
<keywords>George II, Powel, Handel, Welsh harpers, Welsh language, linguistics, Eisteddfod, Taliesin, poetry, bards, Mr. Walters, Dr. Llewelyn</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh Bards by examining the technical structure of their poetry and the natural origins of Welsh music. It follows a historical timeline that recently mentioned the influence of the harper Powel on the composer Handel.</meta>

To an English reader, this may seem like a laborious way of wasting time, but every language has its own unique laws of harmony. The ancient languages of Greece and Rome were not burdened with an overabundance of consonants; they were primarily composed of multi-syllable words and vowel endings. Their poetry, therefore, based its rhythm on "quantity"—the artistic arrangement of long and short syllables. In contrast, the old British language was rich in consonants and made up of single-syllable words, which are incompatible with "quantity." The Bards could only bring the language into harmony by placing its harsher consonants at specific intervals, mixing them with vowels, and repeating or dividing sounds to create a pleasing effect from their structure.

As a result, the rules of poetic composition in this language are so strict and rigorous that they would severely limit a Bard’s genius, if not for the language's natural aptitude for this type of alliterative melody. This melody is as essential to Welsh poetry as harmony is to music, and it constitutes its greatest beauty. To the ears of native speakers, Welsh meter is extremely pleasing and does not restrict the Bard any more than the various rhythmic "feet" restricted Greek and Roman poets <margin>ʳ *Northern Antiquities*, 8vo. London, vol. I. p. 401, etc.</margin>. Traces of <term>Cynghanedd</term> <note>original: *Cynghanedd*; a complex system of internal rhyme and alliteration unique to Welsh poetry</note> or alliteration appear in the poetic remains of the Druids. It was known to the Bards of the sixth century, though they used it sparingly and without set rules. From the Norman Conquest until the death of <term>Llywelyn</term> the Last, the Bards became more strict. From the time of Llywelyn until the reign of Elizabeth I, the laws of alliteration were prescribed and followed with scrupulous exactness. A line that was not perfectly alliterative was condemned by Welsh grammarians as severely as a mistake in syllable length would be by the Greeks or Romans <margin>ˢ *Northern Antiquities*, vol. II. p. 197, etc.</margin>.

The Bards, like other poets, were eager to show off their linguistic wealth. As soon as they realized the extent of their power, they began to wander freely through all the complexities of <term>Cynghanedd</term>. They provided other proofs of an unrivaled system of poetic meter. They were not satisfied with the smooth flow of this couplet, written on a harp:

->Within the hollow of its frame is found<-
->The magic scale of soul-enchanting sound <margin>ᵗ Walter’s *Dissertation on the Welsh Language*, p. 52.</margin>.<-
<note>original Welsh: *Mae mil o leifiau melyfon, / Mal mêl o byd ym mola bon.*</note>

They sought even smoother rhythms and produced examples like the following <term>Englyn i'r Pryf Coppyn</term> <note>original: *Englyn i'r Pryf Coppyn*; an *Englyn* is a traditional Welsh short poetic form, and *Pryf Coppyn* means "Spider"</note>, or *Epigram on the Spider*, which is composed entirely of vowels:

->From his proper egg to weave he goes—and with his webs<-
->From his eggs he weaves,<-
->He weaves it, he weaves it,<-
->And his webs are the yokes of the Lord <margin>ᵘ Rhydderch’s *Welsh Grammar*, p. 141. See this *Englyn* ingeniously answered in another, composed likewise of vowels, by the Rev. Mr. Gronw Owen; *Family Amusement: The Works of the Bards of Anglesey* (original: *Diddanwch Teuluadd, Gwaith Beirdd Môn*), 18vo. Lond. 1763, p. 35.</margin>.<-
<note>original Welsh: *O'i wiw wy i weu ê â, -- a'i weuau / O'i wyau y weua, / E'i weua ei weua, / A'i, weuau yw ieau Jâ.* This is a famous phonetic tour de force in Welsh literature.</note>

In terms of grandeur, the following <term>distich</term> <note>a pair of poetic lines</note> on Thunder could not be surpassed:

->Fire and water struggling together<-
->Are the thunders, those dragons yonder <margin>ᵛ Walter’s *Dissertation*, p. 53.</margin>.<-
<note>original Welsh: *Tân a dŵr yn ymwriaw / Yw'r taranau dreigiau draw.*</note>

But it is exceeded in difficulty by the following <term>Englyn</term>, composed only of vowels and the consonant *r*:

->Cold is the snow on Snowdon—of a kind,<-
->In the air it is frost,<-
->On its surface is the slope of the Lord,<-
->Cold is the "Frozen Hill" <margin>ʷ Rhydderch’s *Welsh Grammar*, p. 141.</margin>.<-
<note>original Welsh: *Oer yw'r eira ar Eryri - - o ryw, / Ar awyr i rew / Ar ei wra yw 'r riw'r Ri, / Oer yw'r 'Rhyriw'.* Eryri is the Welsh name for the Snowdonia mountains.</note>

Such examples should not be read with ridicule or disgust; they were not intended to show off the poet's skill, but rather the unique powers of the language. 

There is still something to be said about Welsh Music. Although the supernatural powers and effects once fabulously attributed to ancient music are now rightly mocked, it is not difficult to imagine that—despite its known simplicity—its association with poetry made it more articulate and expressive. It might have had a much greater impact on the mind and emotions than the artificial melodies and complex harmonies of modern times. The music, as well as the poetry, of Wales was infused with its own unique and original character by the spirit of the country. They sprang from the same soil, drawing their soft and tender rhythms from the delightful valleys, and their bolder, more animated tones from the wild mountain landscapes <margin>ᵂ Whoever wishes to see this idea explored at length may find it ingeniously and philosophically developed—with reference to the native music of Scotland—in *Dr. Beattie's Essays on Poetry and Music*.</margin>.

->deriving<-

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>THE WELSH BARDS.</header>

### THE WELSH BARDS.

And where could the Muses have chosen a happier residence? At times, you are delighted with valleys that are at once wild and beautiful; in other parts, you are astonished by a continuous stretch of dreary, cloud-capped country—"hills whose heads touch heaven"—dark, tremendous precipices, swift rivers roaring over disjointed rocks, black caverns, and gushing waterfalls. Did the extravagant imagination of *Salvator Rosa* <note>original: Salvata Rosa; a 17th-century Italian Baroque painter known for "wild and savage" landscapes</note> ever indulge itself in such grand and wild prospects? Or has *Claude Lorraine's* <note>a French painter known for his idealized, serene landscapes</note> matchless brush excelled the Vale of *Clwyd*?

It is no wonder that the venerable Welsh songs possessed such influence on the minds of our ancestors, when we consider their beautiful and varied changes of style and tempo. Their transitions are as abrupt as the rocky landscapes of the country, and as sudden as the passions of the people.

The most ancient style of Welsh music is the *grave* and *solemn*, which was dedicated to religious purposes and occasions <gloss>x</gloss>. The next, distinct from the former, is fiercely *martial* and *magnificent* <gloss>y</gloss>. Another is mournful and expressive of sorrow, being reserved for elegies and the celebration of the dead <gloss>z</gloss>. Another is of the *pastoral* kind, and perhaps the most agreeable of all; it comes closest to nature and possesses a pleasing simplicity and soothing tranquility, suitable for romantic love <gloss>*</gloss>.

Of these ancient melodies, I have recovered some genuine remains, and their effects are not wholly lost or forgotten. A new era of Welsh-British harmony has risen in our times, and the wonderful things told of it in former ages have already been realized.

> The trembling strings about her fingers crowd,
> And tell their joy for every kiss aloud.
> Small force there needs to make them tremble so;
> Touched by that hand, who would not tremble too?
->Waller.<-

The harp, in the hands of the British fair <gloss>†</gloss> <note>meaning the women of Britain</note>, has acquired new honors and a more irresistible influence. It never produced such transport and enthusiasm when struck by a *Cybelyn* or a *Cadwgan* <note>legendary ancient Welsh harpers</note> as it now excites, assisted by the clear voice and distinguished beauty of our modern female Bards.

->EDWARD JONES.<-

***

<margin>x</margin> The fine old *Psalms*, which are chanted in some of the churches in *Wales*, particularly in those where modern singing styles have not been introduced.
Likewise the *Song of Aedan* <note>original: Côr-Aedan</note>, *Song of the Slender-edge* <note>original: Côr-finfain</note>, *Song of the Wreathed* <note>original: Côr-wrygog</note>, *Song of Alchan* <note>original: Côr-Alchan</note>, *Song of the Frontier* <note>original: Côr-Ffiniwr</note>, *Song of the Column* <note>original: Côr-y-golofn</note>, etc. Some of these *Songs* <note>original: Côr's</note> or holy songs are carefully displayed from an ancient manuscript in the original musical notes, supposed to be Druidical, which the reader will see engraved on a book illustrated in the print of musical instruments later in this volume.

<margin>y</margin> *The Warrior’s Song* <note>original: Triban</note>, or *The Song of the Three Lines*, *The Glamorgan Song* <note>original: Triban Morganwg</note>, *Captain Morgan’s March* <note>original: Mynediad Cadpen Morgan</note>, *The Vibration of the String* <note>original: Erddigan tro'r tant</note>, *The White Lock of Hair* <note>original: Cudyn Gwyn</note>, *The Monk's March* <note>original: Ymdaith Mwngc</note>, *The Queen’s Dream* <note>original: Breuddwyd y Frenhines</note>, *Flowers of the Heath* <note>original: Blodeu'r Grug</note>, *The Break of Day* <note>original: Torriad y Dydd</note>, *Jenkin* <note>original: Sienkyn</note>, *The Bending of the Little Horseshoe* <note>original: Plygiad y Bedol-fach</note>, *Ned Puw's Granddaughter* <note>original: Wyres Ned Puw</note>, *The Spade Head* <note>original: Pen Rhaw</note>, *Farewell, Ned Puw* <note>original: Farwel Ned Puw</note>, etc.

<margin>z</margin> *The Marsh of Rhuddlan* <note>original: Morfa Rhuddlan; a famous lament for a defeat in battle</note>, *The Heavy Heart* <note>original: Y Galon Drom</note>, *David of the White Rock* <note>original: Dafydd Garreg-wen</note>, *Gorddinam*, *The Composition of Gruffudd ab Cynan* <note>original: Consét Gruffudd ab Cynan</note>, *Hard to Depart* <note>original: Anhawdd ymadael</note>, *The Gentle Maid of Anglesey* <note>original: Mwynen Môn</note>, *The Simpleton of the Fingertip* <note>original: Symlen Ben bys</note>, *The Old Tune* <note>original: Yr Hen Dôn</note>, etc.

<margin>*</margin> *Venture, Gwen* <note>original: Mentra Gwen</note>, *The Joyful Drunkards* <note>original: Glân Feddwod mwyn</note>, *The Rising of the Lark* <note>original: Codiad yr Hedydd</note>, *Old Sybil* <note>original: Hên Sibel</note>, *Megan’s Daughter* <note>original: Merch Megan</note>, *The Dimple in Her Cheek* <note>original: Twll yn ei boch</note>, *The Nurse's Tune* <note>original: Tôn y Fammaeth</note>, *The Fair One's Choice* <note>original: Dewis Meinwen</note>, *Following Love* <note>original: Dylyn Serch</note>, *Dafydd ap Gwilym’s Composition* <note>original: Consét Dafydd ab Gwilym</note>, *Lady Owen’s Delight* <note>original: Maldod Arglwyddes Owen</note>, *Jane's Mantle* <note>original: Mantell Siani</note>, *New Year’s Eve* <note>original: Nôs Galan; the tune famously used for "Deck the Halls"</note>, *All Through the Night* <note>original: Ar hŷd y nos</note>, *Over the Stone* <note>original: Tros y Garreg</note>, *Megan Who Lost Her Garter* <note>original: Megen a gollodd ei gardas</note>, *Blossoms of the Thorns* <note>original: Blodeu'r Drain</note>, *The Knot of the Wood* <note>original: Cnott y Coed</note>, *The Hog of the Oak* <note>original: Hob y derif</note>, *The Red Piper's Tune* <note>original: Digan y Pibydd Coch</note>, etc. etc.

<margin>†</margin> "The harp is the favorite instrument of the fair sex, and nothing should be spared to make it beautiful: for it should be a primary goal of mankind to attach women by every means to music, as it is the only amusement that may be enjoyed to excess while the heart still remains virtuous and uncorrupted." *Dr. Burney's History of Music*, volume 1.
"Their business should be to practice merely for the amusement of themselves, their own family, and specific friends, or rather for domestic comfort, which they were designed by providence to promote; namely: to calm rowdy passions—to relieve the anxieties and cares of life—to inspire cheerfulness—to soothe the nerves when irritated by pain, sickness, or labor of mind or body—to soothe the irritability of infancy and old age—and to raise the mind to a feeling and love of order. She who shall improve the natural talents with which women are born for doing all these things will not have misspent her time by applying a few years to music."
->*Stillingfleet's Principle and Power of Harmony*, page 151.<-

<meta>This page continues Edward Jones’s history of Welsh music and poetry, moving from the general description of the landscape and musical styles to the specific tradition of "Pennillion" (traditional sung stanzas) and the oral culture of the Welsh peasantry.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

### ON THE
# WELSH PENNILLION,
## OR,
# EPIGRAMMATIC STANZAS*, and PASTORALS.

->*You shall sing in turn; the Muses love alternate song.* <note>original Latin: "Alternis dicetis. Amant alterna Camœnæ"</note> —VIRGIL.<-

**T**hese have been passed down to us by oral tradition from time immemorial and are still the domestic and everyday poetry of the natives of Wales—a people remarkably sensitive to all the impressions of sorrow, love, and joy.

The memorial verses which, in the time of Julius Caesar <gloss>b</gloss>, were never written down and which the Druid disciples spent so many years learning, were *Pennillion* <note>traditional Welsh stanzas</note>, conveyed in that most ancient meter called *Englyn Milwr* <note>The Soldier’s Stanza</note>.

When the Bards had developed their numerous and favorite meters into a very complex system, those which they rejected <gloss>c</gloss> were left to the "Rustic Muse"—the *Awen* <note>poetic inspiration</note> of the common people. When Wales became an English province, poetry had already spread widely among the lower classes. From that period on, they forgot their former favorite subjects of war and terror and focused instead on love and its related passions of pity and grief; thus, these types of *Pennillion* were naturally kept and admired for the tender beauty they contained.

Eventually, around the reign of Queen Elizabeth I, the official constitutional system of the Bards became entirely extinct in Wales. The only poetry that survived was poured out in spontaneous *Pennillion* around the hearths of farmers and in the cottages of shepherds. What helped keep the poetic spirit of the Welsh peasantry alive, despite every discouragement of foreign rule, was their ancient spirit of hospitality <gloss>d</gloss> and social joy. This brought them together to drink mead, sing, and dance to the harmony of the Harp, Crwth <note>an ancient bowed lyre</note>, Pipe, and Drum. Many of these little "sonnets" have been preserved from very distant times due to their exceptional merit and the affection with which they are remembered. Some of the old English songs, repeated a thousand times, still continue to please, while the popular "lullaby of the day" is echoed for a short time and then forgotten forever. The meters of these stanzas are varied: a stanza contains from three to nine lines, and a line consists of a certain number of syllables, from two to eight. One of these meters is the *Triban*, or Triplet; another the *Awdl Gywydd*, or *Hên ganiad* (The memorial ode of the ancient strain); and another is what English poetry would call the Anapestic <note>a meter with two short syllables followed by one long one</note>. There are several kinds of *Pennill* meters that may be adapted and sung to most of the following tunes, with some parts of a tune occasionally being used as an instrumental bridge. Unlike an English song, a single set of words is not restricted to one tune but is commonly sung to several.

The skill of the *pennill*-singers in this is remarkable. According to the meters of their *pennillion*, they join the tune at the proper place and follow it with wonderful exactness until the bridge or the end. While the harp to which they sing might wander into little variations and embellishments, their singing is not confused but stays true to the main melody. This account explains the state of our music and poetry as described by *Giraldus* <note>Giraldus Cambrensis, or Gerald of Wales, a 12th-century chronicler</note> during his time, when the Welsh were a nation of musicians and poets, when musical bands were common among them, and when their children learned to sing in harmony from their infancy <gloss>e</gloss>.

---

<sup>a</sup> The word *Pennill* is derived from *Pen*, a *Head*: because these stanzas flowed spontaneously from, and were stored in, the head without being written down. *Pennill* may also signify a "brief chapter" or "little subject."
<sup>b</sup> See *Caesar's Commentaries on the Gallic War*, book 6, chapter 8.
<sup>c</sup> "Those are to provide a subject for the Bards to sing upon, whether in *Englynion*, *Unodl union*, *Cywydd*, or any of the twenty-four meters, and not in *Dyri*, *Carol*, or any base songs, which the chief Bards of old did not even think worthy to mention, because there are no rules pertaining to them." <note>original Welsh: "Y rhai hynny sy i roddi testun i'r Beirdd i ganu arno..."</note> *Statute of Gruffudd ab Cynan regarding the holding of an Eisteddfod.*

* This proves that *Pennillion* were then frequently composed and admired.
<sup>d</sup> "Among this people, no beggar is to be found; the houses of all are open for the welcome reception of all guests. They value generosity above all virtues, and the spirit of hospitality is so well understood that the ceremony of offering entertainment to strangers, or of asking for it, is unknown here." <note>original Latin/English: "Among this people there is no beggar..."</note> *Giraldus Cambrensis.*
<sup>e</sup> *The Description of Wales*, chapter 13.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh musical and poetic traditions, focusing specifically on the social context and performance of "Pennillion" (traditional sung stanzas). It describes the historical custom of young men visiting households to sing and the enduring popularity of these musical contests among the Welsh peasantry in the 18th century.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>P E N N I L L I O N.</header>

In his day, it was common for groups of young men, who knew no profession but that of arms, to enter every house they came to without hesitation. There, they enjoyed the free conversation of the young women, joined their voices to the melody of the harp, and spent the day in the most lively celebration <note>f</note>. "Even today, a trace of the ancient minstrelsy survives among our mountains. A number of people of both sexes gather and sit around the harp, singing alternately *Pennillion*, or stanzas of ancient or modern compositions."

> "With charming harmony they introduce
> "Their pleasing song, and awaken high raptures;
> "No voice is exempt, for every voice can well join
> "The melodious part."

"The young people usually begin the night with dancing, and when they are tired, they turn to this form of relaxation. They alternately sing, dance, and drink—not for hours, but for days and weeks—and measure time only by the continuation of their mirth and pleasure. Often, like the modern *Improvvisatori* <note>original Italian: Improvvisatore; performers who compose and sing poems spontaneously</note> of Italy, they sing extemporary verses; a person skilled in this art readily produces a *Pennill* <note>a single stanza</note> in response to the last one that was sung." Many have their memories stored with several hundreds, perhaps thousands, of *Pennillion*, some of which they always have ready as answers for every subject that can be proposed. If their memory should ever fail them, they have the creativity to compose something relevant and appropriate for the occasion. The subjects provide a great deal of laughter: some are humorous, others satirical, but most of them are about love, which, due to the nature of the subject, are the best preserved. They continue singing without interruption, never repeating the same stanza (for that would forfeit the honor of being considered the lead singer), and, like nightingales, they sustain the contest through the night. The audience usually requests the tune; sometimes only a few people sing to it, and sometimes the whole company joins in. But when a group of expert singers assembles, they rarely call for a specific tune, for it is a matter of indifference to them what tune the harper plays. Parishes are often pitted against parishes, and even counties contend with counties, until every hill echoes with the chorus <note>g</note>."

In these rural customs, which are best preserved in the mountainous counties of Merioneth and Caernarvon, we have a distant, pleasing glimpse of ancient innocence and the manners of a "golden age."

->Pleasant places for wine and mead,<-
->The musical strings of a playful tune!<-
<note>original Welsh: "Mannau mwyn am win a medd, / Tannau miwsig tôn maswedd!"</note>

Whoever considers the natural sense and pure passions expressed in these fine little pieces of antiquity—sentiments which many would hope for, but few are able to imitate—together with the sweet and soothing air of our musical compositions (which are mostly in the Lydian mode <note>a musical scale often described as having a soft, elegant, or sensual character</note>), will not wonder that, like our national proverbs, they have been preserved by tradition for so long. The same stanzas are remembered in all the counties of Wales, and the natives are so fond of them that they are constantly chanting them whenever they encounter a harp or a <term>Crwth</term> <note>an ancient Welsh bowed instrument, similar to a lyre or violin</note>. Nor will the reader blame my boldness when, as an expression of tender simplicity, I place them in competition with the moving stories of the Scottish Ballads and the delicate *simplicity* <note>original Greek: αφελεια (apheleia); meaning artlessness or pure simplicity</note> of the Greek Epigrams.

<page-num>32</page-num>
# PENNILLION
<note>original: Pennillion; a traditional Welsh poetic form where stanzas are sung to the harp in a specific counterpoint style</note>

| | |
|----------|----------|
| One can go to many a fair,<br>And walk for three<br>Hours,<br>And love a girl from many a parish,<br>Without knowing who<br>Is the best.<br>It is hard to choose a fair oak tree,<br>Without finding a flaw<br>Somewhere! | From festival to festival, from plain to plain,<br>The curious young man <note>original: swain</note> may wander;<br>A perfect maiden he seeks in vain,<br>To meet his constant love:<br>Frequent and fair, like tall young trees,<br>Whole groups <note>original: bevies</note> throng around;<br>But ah! what young tree among them all,<br>Is found without a flaw! |

---

->**1.**<-

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Turn your face, Gwen, be kind,<br>And listen to your lover's complaint;<br>I know there is no man alive<br>Who does not love the color of your eyes. | Turn, lovely Gwen, be good and kind,<br>And listen to your lover's prayer;<br>Full well I know, there is none so blind,<br>But must adore my charming lady. |

---

<warning>A dark ink blot obscures several words in the right column of the second numbered stanza.</warning>
->**2.**<-

| | |
|----------|----------|
| I am here as you see me,<br>Neither in wealth nor poverty;<br>If you dare to live your life with me,<br>You shall have a share of that life. | Do not despise me for being poor,<br>I am not <unclear>very rich</unclear>, it is true;<br>But if you <unclear>will</unclear> endure my lot,<br>I shall be <unclear>rich</unclear> enough in you! |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| The shape of the harp, the shape of the strings,<br>The shape of the golden tuning key turning;<br>Under his fingers, oh, if only it were<br>The shape of my own true heart! | The harp in Howell's <note>original: Howel</note> arms reclined,<br>Warbles in response to his mind;<br>What joys would thrill this delighted <note>original: ravished</note> breast,<br>To be so pressed to his manly bosom! |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Your color, your form, your hand, your eye,<br>Your fair smile, and your light step;<br>Your poet's gaze, and your quiet speech,<br>Have put my life in danger! | Your color, shape, your eye, your hand,<br>Your nimble step, and enchanting smile;<br>Sweet looks, soft speech, command my life,<br>And nearly did my life beguile <note>original: beguile; here meaning to enchant or lead astray</note>! |

| | |
|----------|----------|
| In Anglesey <note>original: Sîr Fôn</note>, there are harp strings,<br>In the Vale of Clwyd <note>original: Nyffryn Clwyd</note>, there are apple trees;<br>In Flintshire <note>original: Sîr Fflint</note>, there is fire to warm oneself,<br>And a beautiful girl to embrace! | In the Isle of Anglesey <note>original: Mona</note>, melodious notes resound,<br>In the rich Vale of Clwyd, sweet <note>original: nectareous</note> fruits abound;<br>Flintshire's green land conceals the useful ore,<br>Much famed for its minerals, but for lovely women more. |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| My beloved is the flower of the year;<br>April, May, June, as well;<br>Sunlight shining on a shadow,<br>And the finest <note>original: gwenithen; literally "the wheat," used to mean the best of the bunch</note> of the girls. | My love is the blossom of the year,<br>The summer months appear in her;<br>The shade enlightens as she passes,<br>She is the gem of charming girls. |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Place your hand, if you do not believe me,<br>Under my breast, and be careful not to hurt me;<br>You will hear, if you listen,<br>The sound of the little heart breaking. | If you doubt my truth,<br>Place your lovely hand upon my breast;<br>Yet touch it gently, lest you increase the pain<br>Of my fond, dying heart. |

# PENNILLION
<note>original: Pennillion; a traditional Welsh poetic form where stanzas are sung to the harp in a specific counterpoint style.</note>

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Oh, my heart, break if you must break! <note>original: Ow! fy nghalon, torr os torri</note> Why do you suffer so persistently? And waste away little by little, like blue ice on a slippery mountain slope! | Oh, break at once in two, my heart <note>original: in twain</note>, and do not pine with a flow of unceasing pain. Do not decay with gradual woes, as ice on the mountains melts away. |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Though the hair on her head is as yellow as gold, let Gwen—who is the fair color of foam <note>original: Lliw’r ewyn</note>—know this: many a bitter root grows in the garden, even if it bears a beautiful flower! | Even if the curls <note>original: ringlets</note> of her hair compare to radiant gold, that charming girl should know that many lovely flowers that grow from bitter roots also thrive in many a garden! |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| How blessed are the wild birds! <note>original: Gwyn eu byd yr adar gwylltion</note> They can go wherever they wish—sometimes to the sea, and sometimes to the mountains—and return home without being scolded <note>original: ddigerydd</note>. | How happy is the state of the wild bird, flying to the sea or the mountains! True and constant to its mate, it lives and dies free and happy. |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| It is weary to love here and there, but it is also weary to be without that weariness. Of all the most tiring troubles, the worst is to have an unpleasant lover and to love in vain! | To love is a great pain, but it is also a pain to be without it. Of all pains, the greatest pain is to love, and to love in vain. |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| A miser must stay in his cabin, and youth must venture out. Until I reach the grave, I too must follow the gentle, pulling harp strings <note>original: dynnion dannau</note>. | The miser stays in his lonely cell while the young man travels and plays. And I, until death brings my passport, must play the harp's long strings. |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| ->1.<- | ->1.<- |
| A hearth of affection lies within my chest; the heart itself is the fuel for love. That fire will never go out as long as any fuel remains! | My heart is the home of deep desire, and affection fans that gentle fire. Constancy increases the flame that always burns the same! |
| | |
| ->2.<- | ->2.<- |
| And faithfulness is the bellows that blow the fire into a flame. With so much heat, it is no wonder to see the water boiling over my eyes <note>A metaphor for tears</note>! | What wonder then that my throbbing chest is filled with such inner heat? From it, all the melting passions rise and burst in torrents from my eyes. |

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| It is easy to say, "Look, there is Snowdon!" <note>original: daccw’r Wyddfa; the highest mountain in Wales</note> but it can only be crossed slowly. It is easy for the healthy, who feel no pain, to tell the sick to take medicine. | To speak of the sublime peak of Snowdon is far easier than to climb it. Similarly, he who is free from pain and care may tell the sick to wear a smile <note>original: to wear a smile; the Welsh original specifically mentions taking medicine or a cure</note>. |

<vocab>Pennillion, Wyddfa, Snowdon, Welsh poetry, parallel translation, harp, Edward Jones</vocab>

It concludes with a famous proverb comparing the ease of giving advice to the difficulty of climbing Snowdon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the collection of Welsh "Pennillion"—short, traditional stanzas typically sung to the accompaniment of the harp. These verses often focus on themes of love, nature, and human experience, reflecting the oral traditions of the Welsh countryside.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

# PENNILLION
<note>original: Pennillion; a traditional Welsh poetic form where stanzas are sung to the harp in a specific counterpoint style.</note>

*In Hafod Elwy <note>A summer pasture in the Elwy valley</note> the cuckoo does not sing,*
*The voice of the raven*
->*is most common;*<-
*When it is finest in every land,*
*It is truly there*
->*as snow.*<-

Far from Elwy, the cuckoo sings,
And suns adorn the sky;
But there the raven flaps his wings,
And eternal snows lie!

---

*Sometimes sad, sometimes joyful,*
*Sometimes with wealth, sometimes with need;*
*Sometimes with gold and silver enough,*
*Sometimes short of river water!*

Sometimes serious, and sometimes merry;
Sometimes rich, and sometimes needy;
Sometimes stored with gold and silver,
Sometimes short of river water.

---

->I.<-

*I have wished a thousand times,*
*That my breast were made of clear glass,*
*So that the maiden <note>original: Fûn; a fair woman</note> might see*
*That my heart is in captivity.*

->I.<-

How often, moved with emotion, have I said,
"Oh! that my breast of glass were made!"
Then might she see, angelic and fair,
The love her charms have kindled there!

->2.<-

*There never was a girl so beautiful,*
*There never was a girl so fair,*
*There was no one among the daughters of men,*
*Closer than she to breaking my heart.*

->2.<-

There never was a maiden so fair,
Of such a bewitching shape and manner;
There never was of woman kind,
One half so suited to my mind.

---

*Lead is heavy, and stones are heavy,*
*The heart of every lonely person is heavy;*
*The heaviest thing between the sun and moon,*
*Is saying farewell, where there is love!*

Sad and heavy sinks the stone,
Thrown on the lake's smooth surface;
A man oppressed by sorrow's weight
Sadly sinks beneath his fate;
But the saddest thing to tell,
Is to love, and bid farewell!

---

*A churl <note>original: gerlyn; a miserly or rude fellow</note> in his bed is delighted*
*To hear the sound of the spinning wheels!*
*But I—may God look upon me—am delighted*
*To hear the sound of the strings tuning!*

Happy the miser will always be,
To see his wealth increasing around him;
But that which is joyful and pleases me
Is when notes harmonize with crowned voices!

---

*Wind on the sea, and sun on the mountain,*
*Grey stones instead of trees;*
*And seagulls instead of men,*
*Oh! God, how could my heart not break!*

Wild over the sea the tempest flies,
The radiant sun leaves the skies;
Grey stones ruin the naked heath,
And the storm howls loudly and piteously;
Shrill screams the hungry gulls between,
And desolation ruins the scene.
What heart could endure such terrors,
Except for one secure in Your help, my God!

---

*The skin of the lamb is as common in the market*
*As the skin of the sheep;*
*And as common in the churchyard*
*Is the burial of the daughter as the burial of the mother!*

As often in the market the skin of the lamb
As the skin of the sheep <note>original: wether; a castrated ram</note> is seen:
Nor is it more common in churchyards to bury the mother <note>original: dame</note>,
Than her daughter of blooming fifteen.

<meta>This page continues a collection of Welsh *Pennillion*—traditional short stanzas often sung to the harp. The text provides the original Welsh verses followed by an English poetic adaptation. This page explores themes of choosing a partner, the pains of unrequited or secret love, and social commentary on hypocrisy.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>STANZAS <note>original: PENNILLION; the Welsh term for these specific short verses.</note></header>
<sig>7</sig>

# STANZAS

I went to the garden to cut a bouquet;
I refused lavender, I refused the lily,
I refused mint and red roses,
And chose a bouquet of stinging nettles! <note>original Welsh: Myn'd i'r ardd i dorri pwyfi...</note>

For my breast a bouquet choosing,
Every fragrant flower refusing;
I passed the lilies and the roses,
And of the nettle made my bouquets! <note>original: posies. The author notes this refers to the choice of a wife—suggesting a sharp or difficult partner.</note>

---

If I lost my fair love,
There is a crow for a crow,
Somewhere;
Let her live as she pleases,
And may God's will
Be for me too! <note>original Welsh: Os collais i fy nghariad lân...</note>

Should I lose my fairest love,
For a dove there's still a dove,
Somewhere or other to be found;
At heart's ease may she ever be!
Whatever heaven designs for me,
May she in peace and joy abound!

---

The cuckoo does not sing in wintertime;
The harp does not sing without strings;
The heart does not sing to let you know
When there is grief at its bottom! <note>original Welsh: Ni chân côg ddim amser gaua'...</note>

In wintry months the cuckoo will not sing;
Nor will the harp resound without a string;
With one bright thought the heart cannot glow,
Oppressed by grief, and overcome by woe.

---

->1.<-

How happy I would be, if I were so lucky
In the world as to have my choice;
I would choose, instead of wealth,
Beauty of form and perfect love! <note>original Welsh: Gwyn fy myd, na fawn mor happus...</note>

From pleasure's universal stores,
Nor wealth, nor power my heart implores;
But beauty's fair, sincere face,
And faithful love's sincere embrace.

->2.<-

Wealth can be found by saving,
Land can be found by paying for it;
Beauty can be found by searching,
But kindness is only found from "Someone." <note>original Welsh: Fe gair cyfoeth ond cynnilo...</note>

Beauty, too easily sold, may be hired,
And land be purchased, wealth acquired;
But happiness that never was bought,
Must in one fair one's arms be sought.

->3.<-

Someone it will be! And "Someone" again!
And about "Someone" I am meditating!
When I am sleeping most deeply at night,
"Someone" comes and wakes me! <note>original Welsh: Rhywun fydd! a Rhywun etto! This uses "Rhywun"—the Welsh word for "someone"—as a placeholder for a secret lover.</note>

Some fair woman there is, some chosen fair one,
Whose charms are my constant thought and care;
They move my sleeping breast too keenly,
And wake me from the dreams of love.

---

I heard talk, I heard noise,
I heard part of the world blaming others;
I never heard anyone declaring
Much of their own notable faults! <note>original Welsh: Clywais siarad, clywais ddwndro...</note>

Whispers I've heard, and harsh reports,
And half the world reproves the rest,
But none in all this vast resort
Who much of their own faults confessed.

---

I have only two enemies;
How happy I would be if I were between them;
When the maiden <note>original: Meinir; a Welsh name for a fair woman.</note> is in my arms,
The enemies will be the knees! <note>original Welsh: Nid oes imi ond dau elyn... This is a traditional humorous verse about a lover wishing to be closer to his sweetheart, where only their knees keep them apart.</note>

Two enemies alone I fear,
And yet I wish they were more near;
Oh! That I were between the two—
My love must guess—the knees I mean!

<summary>This page features several Welsh Pennillion stanzas with English translations, focusing on the pursuit of "perfect love" over material wealth and the humorous or sorrowful realities of courtship.</summary>
<keywords>Pennillion, Welsh poetry, folk songs, courtship, love vs wealth, social hypocrisy, Meinir</keywords>

<header>STANZAS <note>original: PENNILLION; a traditional Welsh form of short, epigrammatic stanzas often sung to the harp.</note></header>
<sig>3</sig>

# STANZAS

## The Cuckoo’s Song to Merioneth * <note>original: Caniad y Gôg i Feirionydd. Merioneth (Meirionydd) is a historic county in North Wales known for its mountainous landscape.</note>

| <note>Modern Literal Translation of the Welsh</note> | <note>Modernized 18th-Century Poetic Translation</note> |
| :--- | :--- |
| **1.** | **1.** |
| Despite all I have seen beneath the stars, | Whatever I’ve seen beneath the stars, |
| Of the abundance and bravery of countries; | Where fruitful climates abound; |
| Of good beer, and men to serve it, | Of social youths, and flowing jars, |
| And wine on the banks of rivers: | When mirth and wine go round: |
| The best beer and the best food, | All these are only found complete, |
| Were apportioned to Merioneth. | In fair Merioneth's <note>original: Mervinia; the Latin name for the region.</note> sweet retreat. |
| **2.** | **2.** |
| The work is good, the truth must be told, | Merioneth's rocks perhaps are seen, |
| On the hills of the county of Merioneth; | To threaten want and scarcity; |
| A cold view of the worst we could have; | Cold and barren, void of green; |
| Yet she is still full of joy: | Yet full of joy and mirth; |
| Who would expect the cuckoo to sing, | Who expects to hear the nightingale, |
| In a peat-bog on the mountain? | Chanting on the mountains all the year? |
| **3.** | **3.** |
| Who is so fair of face and appearance, | Where can you find greater beauty? |
| But wonderful in the villages? | Each villager has charms! |
| Who is in every good household skill, | Discretion is joined to the housewife, |
| Bound together with one another? | Which warms the pleased beholder: |
| Who is nearly stealing my memory? | In you, Merioneth, dwell the fair, |
| The maidens of the region of Merioneth. | Who rule all hearts, or cause despair! |
| **4.** | **4.** |
| Fair is the salmon in the lake, | How bright is the salmon in the stream? |
| This is nothing new; | How beautiful the thrush? |
| Fair is the thrush in her house, | With expanded wing she seems to gleam, |
| Spreading out her wings: | All sparkling in the bush: |
| Fairer still, if I tell the truth, | And yet how far the maids excel, |
| Are the maidens of the land of Merioneth. | Who in Merioneth’s valleys dwell? |
| **5.** | **5.** |
| Dear to the birds of the world, | As sweet as to the feathered kind, |
| Is their freedom through the woods; | Is ranging through every grove; |
| Dear to an infant is the milk | As sweet as to the infant's mind, |
| Of its nurse, exceptionally happy; | To sip the milk they love; |
| Oh! I would not say for my life, | If I could, I would reveal to you, |
| How dear Merioneth is. | How sweet, Merioneth, you are to me. |
| **6.** | **6.** |
| Pleasant is a Harp within a house, | O tuneful Harp! Melodious sound! |
| Where there is a happy family; | When friends are united; |
| Everyone with their stanza in their turn, | The odes alternately go round, |
| Without mention of the miser's purse: | Unthinking of the miser’s worry. |
| Pleasant the song, around the fire, | How sweet their voices round the fire, |
| Of the fair maidens of Merioneth. | When fair Merioneth women join the lyre! |
| **7.** | **7.** |
| Although my body is in the world's bustle, | Although I am lost in pleasure’s maze, |
| Walking through the countries, | And wander to find new joys; |
| Finding pleasure by sea and land, | Commanding what sea and land can boast, |
| Truly I find no peace; | My mind is still uneasy: |
| Going home is necessary for me: | To you, Merioneth, I will return; |
| My soul is in Merioneth. | My soul for you does ever burn. |

---

\* This poem <note>original: sonnet; used here in the general sense of a short poem or song.</note> was composed by the late Lewis Morris, Esq. <note>A famous Welsh hydrographer, poet, and antiquary (1701–1765).</note> and was translated by the late William Vaughan, Esq. of Cors y Gedol <note>A prominent landowner and Member of Parliament (1707–1775).</note>.

<summary>A seven-stanza poem titled "The Cuckoo’s Song to Merioneth," celebrating the beauty, hospitality, and women of the county of Merioneth. It includes both a literal translation of the Welsh verses and a poetic English adaptation by William Vaughan.</summary>
<keywords>Merioneth, Meirionydd, Lewis Morris, William Vaughan, Welsh poetry, Pennillion, local pride, Welsh harp, cuckoo, pastoral poetry</keywords>

| Moes Erddigan a chanu,<br>Dwg i’n gerdd dēg, Awen gu,<br>Trwy ’r Dolydd taro’r Delyn,<br>Oni bo’r jâs yn y Bryn;<br>O gywair Dant, a gyr di<br>Awr orhoen i Eryri! | Wake, sweet Muse, some golden strain,<br>Voice and string, and over the plain<br>Strike the Harp, whose echoes thrill,<br>Piercing and shaking the distant hill;<br>Far along the winding vale<br>Send the sounds, until every breeze<br>From the bright harmonic string<br>Brings many a tone of rapture,<br>And to *Snowdon* <note>original: Eryri</note> waft on high<br>An hour of tuneful ecstasy! |
| --- | --- |
| Mi âf oddiyma i’r Hafod Lom,<br>Er bōd yn drom<br>Fy ſiwrnai;<br>Mi gaf yno ganu cainge,<br>Ac eiſte’ ar fainge,<br>Y finnai;<br>Ac ond odid dyna’r fan,<br>Y byddaf dan<br>Y borau. | Though the journey is long, I believe,<br>Yet from here to *Hafod Lom* <note>original: Hafod Lom; literally "The Bare Summer-Dwelling," a common name for a farmhouse.</note> I will go;<br>There, chanting many a tuneful piece,<br>I will sit safe in the chimney corner,<br>And perhaps on that happy doorstep,<br>The morning's return shall find me still. |
| --- | --- |
| Rhaid i bawb newidio bŷd,<br>Fe wŷr pob ebud<br>Angall;<br>Pa waeth marw o gariad pûr,<br>Na marw o ddolur<br>Arall? | The stage of life we all must leave,<br>And death will yield us ease;<br>We may as well die from pure love<br>As from some more lingering disease. |
| --- | --- |
| Gwna Hafôd clymmedig,<br>Ac adail o goedwig;<br>A thyn y glau ewig i glywed y Gôg<br>A newid yn ffyddlon,<br>Cuſanau ’n gyfonion,<br>Tan dirion coed irion cadeiriog. | Now raise the twining arbour,<br>Now prepare the green seat;<br>And woo your fair and gentle love<br>To hear the Cuckoo in the grove:<br>Range through the smiling season,<br>And with faithful lips exchange<br>Mutual kisses with the maiden,<br>Seated in the enfolding shade. |
| --- | --- |
| O! f’arglwydd Dduw cyfion, pa beth ſy’n eich brŷd,<br>A’i dringo pôb cangen, o’r bôn hyd y brîg?<br>Y brigyn fydd uchel a’r codwm fydd fawr,<br>Fe geir eich cwmpeini, pan ddeloch i lawr! | Ye Gods! Is it possible you intend,<br>With undaunted courage, to climb this tree?<br>The branches are high, and the fall is painful;<br>Your old friends might see you once more [when you hit the ground]! |
| --- | --- |
| D’accw Llwyn o fedw gleiſion,<br>D’accw’r Llwyn ſy’n torri ’ngalon;<br>Nid am y llwyn yr wy’n ochneidio,<br>Ond am y Ferch a welais ynddo! | See where the green grove of birches grows,<br>That grove so fatal to my heart’s rest:<br>Yet I do not sigh in such despair for the grove itself,<br>But for the maiden I saw (and fell in love with) there. |
| --- | --- |
| Ond ydyw hyn ryfeddod<br>Fod dannedd gwraig yn darfod;<br>A thra bo’n ei genau chwyth, ni dderfydd byth mo’i thafod. | A woman’s charms will pass away,<br>Her eyes grow dim, her teeth decay;<br>But while she breathes the living air,<br>It is strange that her tongue should never fail. |

<header>STANZAS <note>original: PENNILLION; traditional short Welsh verses often sung to the harp.</note></header>

->1.<-
*Carefree is the bird,*
*He neither sows nor reaps a single grain;*
*Without any care in the world, except singing all year long!*

->2.<-
*He eats his supper tonight*
*Without knowing where he will find his dinner;*
*That is the way he lives, leaving it to God to provide!*

->3.<-
*He sits on the branch*
*Looking at his wing,*
*Without a single penny in his purse, managing to be happy!*

---

->1.<-
*Blithe is the bird who wings the plain,*
*Neither sows, nor reaps, a single grain;*
*Whose only labor is to sing,*
*Through Summer, Autumn, Winter, Spring.*

->2.<-
*At night his little meal he finds,*
*Nor heeds what fare may next betide,*
*The change of seasons he does not mind,*
*But for his wants lets Heaven provide.*

->3.<-
*Often on the branch he perches gay,*
*Often on his painted wing looks he,*
*And, penniless, renews his song,*
*Rejoicing in unbounded glee.*

---

*My darling, your two little eyes,*
*I know that quicksilver is in them;*
*They play in your head*
*Like the stars on a bright night!*

---

*I have repented a thousand times,*
*For the act of speaking too many words;*
*There were never such dangers,*
*From speaking less than enough.*

---

*Oh my darling, come at my request,*
*To listen to the voice*
*Of the birds;*
*There is the fairest glade ever,*
*Under the shade of the hazel-wood*
    *of Llangar.* <note>Llangar is a parish and former village in Merionethshire, Wales.</note>

---

*The very nature of my excellent maiden*
*Is to refuse and give in all at once;*
*To cry for peace, yet allow herself to be felt;*
*To say "stop"—and yet let it happen!*

---

*There is not much seeking for a girl,*
*Nor even for a girl of natural virtue;*
*In every place, the strong and the weak*
*Are seeking for money!*

---

*When a man passes forty years of age,*
*Even though he is like trees*
    *In leaf;*
*There will be the sound of the keys of the Grave,*
*Causing his appearance*
    *To change!*

---

*The tender Harp is like*
*A fair girl with her sweet-scented skin;*
*By touching her in private,*
*She becomes sweeter and sweeter.*

---

*If you go to the woods to cut a willow wand,*
*Remember to be wise, my boy;*
*After finding it and starting to weave it,*
*See how many a person fails.*

---

*My Lord God, what is this,*
*That I can do nothing but*
    *Think?*
*Where a youth is most in love,*
*No girl wants*
    *Any of him.*

---

*A loving maiden is like*
*A naughty boy in a neighbor’s house;*
*“Do you want food?” “No, I don’t,”*
*And yet, for all that, he is dying for it!*

---

*Gentle and gentle, and very gentle is a girl,*
*And very gentle where she gives her love;*
*Where a girl gives her love first,*
*That is a love that never grows cold.*

---

*Woe to him who carries a load of beer*
*In his belly just to be drunk;*
*The heaviest load of all loads,*
*It is a load of sins!*

---

*This is the mother of wrong, and lies,*
*Murder, theft, and lewdness;*
*It makes the strong weak, and the weak weaker,*
*The fool foolish, and the fool even foolisher!*

<header>STANZAS <note>original: PENNILLION; traditional short Welsh verses often sung to the accompaniment of a harp.</note></header>

# STANZAS

*While I had a warm welcome and full shelter,*
*I was judged to be exceptionally wise;*
*I became a fool when I left it behind—*
*Graceless, senseless, heavy-headed, and foolish.*
*My dear companions have turned their backs on me;*
*The poor things no longer see a fine young man.*
*Without a word of joking, if they could do so easily,*
*They would hide from my sight in the shadow of a fern!*

---

*Oh, how warm is the breast*
*Of the slender maiden;*
*Oh, how sweet is the*
*Grove of the Clover;* <note>original: Llwyn Meillionen; a popular Welsh tune and place name.</note>
*Oh, how sweet are the kisses,*
*Given with love and gentle words!*

---

*Robin Redbreast came to the doorstep*
*With his wing shivering in the cold;*
*And he said quite playfully,*
*“It is cold; snow is coming.”*

---

*Whether old or young, wise or foolish,*
*The girls are getting married, while I am left behind;*
*Why do the young men see me as so plain,*
*When I am as fair as the girls who find husbands?*

---

*While walking through the churchyard,*
*Where many bodies rest,*
*I struck my foot against my sweetheart’s grave,*
*And I felt my heart turn over!*

---

*I have heard that in some way,*
*There are eight parts of speech in this world;*
*And—to their discredit—the women*
*Have taken seven of those eight parts for themselves!* <note>A traditional humorous observation on women's talkativeness common in period literature.</note>

---

*It is wearisome to dance on the points of an oak branch,*
*And wearisome is the heartache!*
*But it is more wearisome still to lose a sweetheart*
*When she herself is willing!*

---

*The blackbird laughs in the grove;*
*No one plows or sows for her;*
*No one is happier than she!*

---

*Gold comes to an end, and silver ends;*
*Sweetness ends, and silk ends;*
*Every fine garment ends—*
*Yet despite all this, longing <note>original: hiraeth; a deep, nostalgic longing or yearning for a person, place, or time.</note> never ends!*

---

*If I have lost my best love,*
*Let the trees lose their blossoms,*
*Let the birds lose their song—*
*May God preserve the fruits of the earth.*

---

*I gave my love to the flower of the valley,*
*And she gave her love to someone else;*
*That man gave his love to another—*
*Which of the three of us is the most foolish?*

---

*Lead is my penance—*
*Where is my fair one?*
*Wounds and grief,*
*I have found only terror!*

---

*Gentle Jane,*
*Slender Jane,*
*Elegant Jane,*
*Dear Jane,*
*Poor Jane tonight;*
*Jane of the sweet voice,*
*Jane of the lovely speech,*
*Jane who is so right for me to talk with:*
*As long as the crow’s call is high in the air,*
*Jane will never be forgotten!*

---

*Venture forth, maiden, and follow me—*
*Will you receive*
*Excellent love?*
*I will not pressure you even by the width of a die,*
*Unless you choose*
*To come!*

---

*There is many an apple on the tip of a tree,*
*With a yellow skin;*
*But the flesh under the skin is not worth*
*The pain of climbing for it!*
*That one, before the end of summer,*
*Is the most likely and certain to turn sour.*

---

*Across the sea is my heart!*
*Across the sea are my sighs!*
*Across the sea is my beloved,*
*Who is in my thoughts every minute!*

---

*The courting has ended, the walking has ended;*
*The maiden has found her satisfaction.*
*I, too, have cast off my mourning*
*For every journey I took in vain.*

---

<page-num>41</page-num>

## THE MUSICAL INSTRUMENTS OF THE WELSH.

<margin>
^a “Let the Roman applaud you with the Lyre, the Barbarian with the Harp, the Greek with the Achilles-style lyre, and let the British **Crwth** sing.” <note>original Latin: *Romanusque Lyra plaudat tibi, Barbarus Harpâ, Graecus Achilliaca, Crotta Britanna canat.* Venantius Fortunatus, Book 7, Poem 8. Venantius Fortunatus (c. 530–600 AD) was a Latin poet and bishop; this is one of the earliest historical mentions of the British *Crwth*.</note>
</margin>

**T**HE musical instruments used in ancient *Wales* are as different from those of other nations as their music and poetry are ^a.

These instruments are five in number: the <term>*Telyn*</term> (Harp), the <term>*Crwth*</term> (Crowd), the <term>*Pibgorn*</term> (Pipe), the <term>*Tabwrdd*</term> (Tabret or drum), and the <term>*Corn Buelin*</term> (Cornet or Bugle Horn). An accurate representation of these is attempted in the "Trophy" <note>In 18th-century illustration, a "Trophy" refers to a decorative arrangement of related objects—in this case, the musical instruments shown in the engraving.</note> illustration at the beginning of this chapter.

<margin>
^b *Genesis*, chapter 4.
</margin>

The Harp—the most important of those I have listed, and indeed the queen of all instruments—originates from very ancient times. *Jubal*, in the *Hebrew* tradition, is called the father of all those who play the Harp and the Organ ^b <note>original: "Harp and Organ"; in early biblical translations, these terms referred generally to stringed and wind instruments.</note>. King *David* introduced it into his choir and praised the Lord on an instrument of ten strings.

Among the *Greeks*, the Harp is also very ancient and is described as having the triangular shape of the Greek letter Δ (**Delta**). The honor of its invention is given to *Mercury*, who, after finding a dead shell-fish <note>Specifically a tortoise shell, according to myth.</note> on the shore of the *Nile*, turned the shell into a Lyre. He fitted it with strings and a <term>jugum</term> <note>A cross-bar or "yoke" used to stretch or slacken the strings for tuning.</note> to tune them. According to *Diodorus Siculus* <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BC.</note>, this Lyre had only three strings, matching the three seasons of the year—Spring, Summer, and Winter—which were the only seasons the *Greeks* recognized. *Boethius* <note>A 6th-century philosopher whose work *De Institutione Musica* was a standard music textbook for centuries.</note> quotes some authorities who...

<sig>M</sig>
<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword "assign" indicates the next page begins with that word.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the five primary historical musical instruments of Wales and begins a scholarly history of the harp, tracing its origins from Biblical figures like Jubal and David to ancient Greek mythology.</summary>
<keywords>Welsh musical instruments, Telyn, Harp, Crwth, Pibgorn, Tabwrdd, Corn Buelin, Mercury's Lyre, Jubal, King David, Diodorus Siculus, Boethius</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the history of stringed instruments, transitioning from the ancient harp to its specific development in Wales, and introducing the Crŵth.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>MUSICAL INSTRUMENTS OF THE WELSH.</header>

...assign it four strings, in imitation of the music of the spheres <note>original: "mundane music." This refers to the ancient "musica mundana," the belief that the movements of celestial bodies and the four elements created a form of silent, mathematical music.</note> of the four elements. *Homer*, *Pindar*, *Horace*, *Virgil*, *Nicomachus*, and *Lucian* agree that it had seven strings, a number which corresponds with that of the planets. Such was the Lyre which *Mercury* invented and gave to *Orpheus*; after his death, it was hung up by the people of *Lesbos* in *Apollo's* temple. *Pythagoras* found it afterwards in a temple in *Egypt* and improved it by the addition of an eighth string. *Timotheus the Milesian* added four new ones to the original seven. *Josephus*, in his *Jewish Antiquities*, mentions a Lyre with twelve strings. In the time of *Anacreon*, the number had become much greater, for that poet himself informs us that he sang across the whole range of the *Twenty Strings* <sup>c</sup>.

Nevertheless, we find that the *Telyn*, or Welsh Harp, was always unique to our Bards; though, probably, there was no difference between the Harp in its ancient primitive form and the Greek Lyre. For *Diodorus Siculus* records that the Celtic Bards played on instruments like lyres <note>original Greek: *ὀργάνων ταῖς λύραις ὁμοίων* (orgánōn taîs lýrais homoíōn)</note> <sup>d</sup>.

In the time of the Welsh Princes, an hereditary Harp was preserved with great care and veneration in the household of every Prince and Lord, to be bestowed successively on the Bards of the family; it was as indispensable among the possessions of a gentleman <sup>e</sup> as a coat of arms.

The triple or modern Welsh Harp has three rows of strings: the two outside rows are tuned in unison, while the middle row provides the flats and sharps. Its range extends to five octaves. Some of its current features were likely additions from later centuries. This celebrated instrument has been recently improved by the invention of pedals, which change its key without the need for retuning, rendering it much less complicated and inconvenient by reducing it to a single row of strings.

In expression and variety, the Harp has no rival. This will be acknowledged by all who know how the heart is soothed by its delicate and softer sounds, as well as animated by its more powerful and brilliant tones. This is elegantly expressed by a Welsh Bard in the following stanza:

| | |
|---|---|
| *Difyrwch, didrwch, didrais, (Tawelaidd)* | O Harp! within your magic cells |
| *Yw Telyn byfrydlais;* | Light, airy glee and pleasure dwells, |
| *Crŷ’ odlau, cywir adlais,* | And gentle rapture rings; |
| *Nefolaidd wiw hwyfaidd lais!* | While clear-voiced echo sends around |
| | The heavenly gale of tuneful sound, |
| | From all the according strings. |

The <term>Crŵth</term> <note>A traditional Welsh bowed string instrument, often considered a precursor to the violin.</note> is second in rank among Welsh musical instruments. I believe it to be the parent of the violin. It has a most agreeable melody and was frequently used as a tenor accompaniment to the Harp. It has now become extremely rare in *Wales*. Its length is 20 1/2 inches, and its breadth at the bottom is 9 1/2 inches, tapering to 8 inches toward the top. It is 1 7/10 inches thick, and the fingerboard measures 10 inches in length.

The <term>Crŵth</term> has a much wider range than the violin and is capable of great perfection; therefore, it deserves to be studied. It has six strings:

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. | *Y Crâf-dant.* | } | The sharp string |
| 2. | *A’i Fyrdon.* | } | And its bass. |
| 3. | *Byrdon y Llorf-dant.* | } | Accompaniment to the bass string. |
| 4. | *Y Llorf-dant.* | } | The bass string. |
| 5. | *Y Cywair-dant.* | } | The key note, |
| 6. | *A’i Fyrdon.* | } | And its bass. |

The strings of the <term>Crŵth</term> explained, and the usual method of tuning it:

<meta>Musical notation diagram with tuning instructions: "Tune the 1st string." "Then tune the 5th string to it, thus." "Tune the 6th string to the 5th string." "The 2nd string to the first." "The 3rd string to the fifth." "And the 4th string to the 3rd."</meta>

Two or three of the lower strings of the <term>Crŵth</term> are often struck with the thumb and serve as a bass accompaniment to the notes sounded with the bow, somewhat in the manner of the <term>Baryton</term> <note>A multi-stringed bowed instrument of the gamba family, famous for having extra strings that could be plucked with the thumb.</note>. The bridge of this instrument is simple yet curious, serving also as a soundboard; the reader will observe this if he looks at the illustration of it in the "Trophy" engraving.

---

<sup>c</sup> See the *Prolegomena* to *Burney’s* edition of *Anacreon*, and *Grassineau’s Musical Dictionary*.
<sup>d</sup> I have seen some antique Harps in *Wales* with 18 or 20 strings, others with 25 or 30.
<sup>e</sup> See King *Hywel's* Laws, published by Dr. *Wotton*.

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh musical instruments, following the description of the Harp and the standard Crŵth on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
# MUSICAL INSTRUMENTS OF THE WELSH.

There was likewise a <term>Crŵth Trithant</term> <note>original: "three-stringed Crŵth"</note>, which was the ancient **Bass Viol**. The performers on this instrument were not held in the same estimation and respect as the Bards of the Harp and the standard <term>Crŵth</term>. This was because the three-stringed <term>Crŵth</term> did not require equal skill, and consequently, its musical power was less significantly felt.

The <term>Pibgorn</term>, or **Hornpipe**, is so called because both of its ends are made of horn. When blown, the air passes through it and vibrates the tongue of a reed concealed within. It has seven holes and measures about 19 inches in length. Its tone is a medium between the flute and the clarinet and is remarkable for its melody. This rural pipe is unique to the **Isle of Anglesey**, where it is played by shepherds; it adds greatly to the innocent delight of pastoral life.

The <term>Tabwrdd</term> (**Drum** or **Tabret**) was used either in war or to accompany other instruments in concerts, festivals, and similar occasions. We find in the laws of King <note>King Hywel Dda (Howel the Good), a 10th-century Welsh ruler famous for codifying Welsh law.</note> **Howel** that harps and voices were principally used by the ancient Welsh to inspire courage before a battle. There is reason to think, however, that <term>Crŵths</term>, pipes, and drums were used for the same purpose.

The last instrument, which perhaps should have been mentioned earlier, is the <term>Corn Buelin</term> or **Bugle Horn**. This instrument was sometimes called *Corn Hirlas*, *Corn Cyweithas*, and *Corn Cychwyn*; names which signify the **Long Blue Horn**, **The Horn of the Household**, and the **Marching Horn**. It was made from—and received its general name from—the horn of the buffalo, bugle, or wild ox <note>See the Laws of King Howel.</note>, an animal that was formerly common in **Wales**. In the time of King **Howel**, it was the office of the Master of the Royal Hounds to sound his bugle horn in war, for a march, and to give the alarm and signal for battle. He likewise used it in hunting to animate the hunters and the dogs and to call the pack together. The Master of the Hounds had the same power of protection (sanctuary) within the sound of his horn while he was hunting as the Chief Bard possessed while performing on his Harp. When his oath was required in a court of justice, he swore by his horn. By the old Welsh hunting laws <note>Published at the end of Dr. Davies’s Dictionary.</note>, it was decreed that every person carrying a horn was obliged to know the **Nine Chases**; if he could not give a proper account of them, he would lose his horn. There were three bugle horns belonging to the King: his drinking horn, the horn for calling together the household, and the horn of the Master of the Hounds <note>Howel’s Laws.</note>.

This instrument occasionally had lids at its ends and served as the cup from which our ancestors drank mead. They valued it as much for this purpose as for its shrill and warlike sound. The jovial horn was sometimes a subject for the Welsh poets. There is a very fine, spirited poem in the Reverend Mr. Evans’s *Specimens of the Welsh Bards* entitled *Hirlas Owain* (**The Long Blue Horn of Owain**), composed by **Owain Cyfeiliog**, Prince of Powys. It is elegantly translated in Mr. Pennant’s last *Tour in Wales*. If I may take the liberty to borrow some lines from it, they will give the reader an idea of how our famous ancestors used to feast after battle in days of yore:

> “Fill the *Hirlas Horn*, my boy,
> Nor let the tuneful lips be dry
> That warble **Owain’s** praise;
> Those walls with warlike spoils are hung,
> And open wide his gates are flung
> In **Cambria’s** <note>original: "Cambria"; the Latin name for Wales.</note> peaceful days.
> 
> This hour we dedicate to joy;
> Then fill the *Hirlas Horn*, my boy,
> That shines just like the sea;
> Whose azure handle, tipped with gold,
> Invites the grasp of **Britons** bold,
> The sons of liberty.
> 
> Fill it higher still, and higher,
> Mead will noblest deeds inspire.
> Now the battle’s lost and won,
> Give the horn to **Gronwy’s** son;
> Put it into **Gwgan’s** hand,
> Bulwark of his native land,

<column-break/>

> Guardian of **Sabrina’s** <note>original: "Sabrina"; the Latin name for the River Severn.</note> flood,
> Who often has dyed his spear in blood.
> When they hear their chieftain’s voice,
> Then his gallant friends rejoice;
> But when to fight he goes, no more
> The festive shout resounds on **Severn’s** winding shore.
> 
> Fill the gold-tipped horn with speed,
> (We must drink, it is decreed.)
> Badge of honor, badge of mirth,
> That calls the soul of music forth!
> As you wish to prolong your life,
> Fill it with **Metheglin** <note>original: "Metheglin"; a Welsh spiced mead (meddyglyn).</note> strong.
> 
> Pour out the horn (though he does not ask for it),
> And heave a sigh on **Morgan’s** early grave;
> Doomed in his clay-cold tenement to rot,
> While we revere the memory of the brave.”

<summary>This page describes various Welsh instruments beyond the harp, including the three-stringed Crŵth, the Pibgorn (hornpipe), and the Tabwrdd (drum). It focuses extensively on the Bugle Horn (Corn Buelin), detailing its use in war, hunting, and as a ceremonial drinking vessel for mead.</summary>

<keywords>Crŵth Trithant, Pibgorn, Tabwrdd, Bugle Horn, Corn Hirlas, King Howel, Hywel Dda, Owain Cyfeiliog, Hirlas Owain, Metheglin, mead, Welsh musical history, Isle of Anglesey</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bugle Horn (Corn Buelin), also known as the Hirlas Horn, concluding with a poetic tribute and a description of a specific historical artifact.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
# MUSICAL INSTRUMENTS OF THE WELSH.

Fill the horn with foaming liquor,
Fill it up, my boy, be quicker;
Hence away, despair and sorrow!
Time enough to fight tomorrow.
Let the brimming goblet smile,
And *Ednyfed*’s care beguile;
Gallant youth, unused to fear,
Master of the broken spear,
And the arrow-pierced shield,
Brought with honor from the field.
Like a hurricane is He,
Bursting on the troubled sea.
See their spears stained <note>original: "distain'd"</note> with gore!
Hear the din of battle roar.
Bucklers, swords, together clashing,
Sparkles from their helmets flashing!
Hear you not their loud alarms?
Hark! they shout—to arms! to arms!

Thus were *Garthen*’s plains defended,
The *Maelor* fight began and ended.
There two princes fought, and there
Was *Morach Vorvran*’s feast exchanged for retreat <note>original: "rout"</note> and fear.

Fill the horn: it is my delight,
When my friends return from fight,
Champions of their country’s glory,
To record each gallant story.—
To *Ynyr*’s comely offspring fill,
Foremost in the battle still;
Two blooming youths, in counsel wise <note>original: "sage"</note>,
As heroes of a more mature age;
In peace and war alike renowned;
Be their brows with garlands crowned,
Adorned with glory let them shine,
The ornament and pride of *Ynyr*’s ancient line!”

I was fortunate to find one of these celebrated Horns at *Penrhyn* near *Bangor* in *Caernarvonshire*, formerly the seat of the *Griffiths*. By the initials and a crest on the horn, I find that it belonged to *Sir Rhys Gruffudd*; and afterward to his valiant son *Sir Piers Gruffudd*, who was living in 1598. I made a correct drawing of it, which I have had engraved in the "Trophy" <note>This likely refers to a decorative engraved illustration or frontispiece in the original book where various instruments are displayed together.</note>, where the reader will see it hanging on the top of the Harp. The original is the most elegant antique I have ever seen: it is tipped with carved silver and decorated with a beautiful silver chain <gloss>i</gloss>.

---

| | |
|----------|----------|
| <gloss>i</gloss> Its dimensions are the following: | The diameter of the drinking end: 2 1/2 inches. |
| The diameter of the semi-circle: 13 1/4 inches. | The diameter of the blowing end: rather above 5/8 of an inch. |
| The total length of the semi-circle: 21 1/2 inches. | And it contains about half a pint. |

<sig>.1</sig>

<header>Captain Morgan’s March <note>original Welsh: Mynediad Câdpen Morgan</note>. 47</header>

## Captain Morgan’s March <note>original Welsh: Mynediad Câdpen Morgan</note>

**Majestically** <note>original Italian: Maestoso</note>

> Did you not hear the noise from afar? **HARLECH** <note>original: HURLECH</note> led the battle;
> 
> **ENGLAND**'s <note>original: LLOEGER</note> terror, **WALES**'s <note>original: CYMRY</note> shield, **HARLECH** cleared the defeated field.

---

2
Wolves that hear their young ones cry
Attack their hunters more tamely:
Harvests, falling prey to the flames,
Perish slower <note>original: flower (likely an OCR error for "slower")</note> still than they.

3
Yours, swift **CYNAN**, is the race
Where we trace the warrior's lineage:
Brave **TYNDAETHWY**, boast to claim
**HARLECH** as your even braver son.

4
Swift is the rapid eagle's flight,
Darting from his airy height:
Swifter still was **HARLECH**'s winged speed
When he caused the battle to bleed.

5
Strong is the deep stream of the **OGWEN** River
Thundering down its craggy cliff:
Stronger still is **HARLECH**'s matchless might,
Raging through the ranks of battle.

6
**SNOWDON**'s <note>original: WYDDFA</note> snows, driven for ages,
Melt before the lightning bolts of heaven:
So, blasted by **HARLECH**'s eye,
The hearts of heroes melt and die.

7
Stung with terror, the deer fly
When the pack's wild uproar bursts near:
So, dismayed by **HARLECH**'s voice,
Hosts of heroes shrank and fled.

8
"Raise your harps, raise your voices,
Always grateful in **HARLECH**'s praise:
**HARLECH** guards the plains of **GWYNEDD** <note>original: GWYNEDDIA</note>,
Bloody **HENRY** thirsts for it in vain.

9
Strike louder, and louder yet,
Until the echoing caves repeat:
"**HARLECH** guards the plains of **GWYNEDD**,
Bloody **HENRY** thirsts for it in vain.

10
Stay far away, far from **WALES**,
Rage on, you fiend of horrid war;
**WALES**'s strength in **HARLECH**'s spear
Mocks the rage that threatens here!

11
For long, too long, a band of ruffians—
Murderous **SAXONS**—plundered the land;
**HARLECH** rose, and the destruction is over.
Murderous **SAXONS** plunder no more.

12
**ENGLAND** now shall feel in turn
That **WALES**'s vengeance can also burn;
The thirst for blood and the thirst for loot
Shall recoil upon the plunderers' heads.

13
Do doves fly when hawks <note>original: Kites</note> pursue them?
Cowards! So we rush upon you:
Flight shall fail you, and no force shall withstand us;
Death and horror shall fill your land." —

I am much indebted to the Reverend Mr. Lambert for this animated and faithful translation of the poem by **Meirion Goch of Snowdonia** <note>original: Meirion Goch o Eryri</note>.

<vocab>Mynediad Câdpen Morgan: Captain Morgan’s March (or Entrance). Hurlech: Harlech, a coastal town in North Wales famous for its castle. Cynan: A reference to the royal lineage of Gwynedd. Tyndaethwy: A district (commote) on the Isle of Anglesey. Ogwen: A river in Carnarvonshire. Wyddfa: The Welsh name for Snowdon, the highest mountain in Wales. Gwyneddia: Gwynedd, the historic kingdom of North Wales. Henry: Likely a reference to King Henry IV or Henry VI during the border wars or the Wars of the Roses. Lloeger: The Welsh word for England. Saxons: A common term used by Welsh bards to refer to the English. Meirion Goch o Eryri: Meirion the Red of Snowdonia, a traditional Welsh poet.</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a martial poem titled "Captain Morgan's March," translated from the original Welsh of Meirion Goch. The verses celebrate the military prowess of a hero associated with Harlech and the defense of North Wales against English (Saxon) incursions.</summary>
<keywords>Captain Morgan's March, Harlech, Welsh poetry, Meirion Goch, Snowdonia, Welsh-English conflict, Harlech Castle, Gwynedd, folk song, martial lyrics</keywords>
