<page-num>50</page-num>

# Rhuddlan Marsh <note>original: *Morfa Rhuddlan*</note>

## Elegiac <note>An "elegiac" piece is a song or poem of serious reflection, typically a lament for the dead.</note>
->The 2nd and 5th variations may be played to accompany the voice<-

<note>Musical notation with interlined lyrics follows</note>

Fair on old Severn's <note>original: *Havren*, the Welsh name for the River Severn</note> bank, the modest violet blooms, and wide the scented air its breath perfumes.

Bright shines the glorious sun amidst the heaven, when from its cheering orb the clouds are driven;

A form more beautiful still adorned the flood: Gwendolen's fatal form, of Llewelyn's blood!

---

->**2**<-
> For her in arms opposed,
> Contending warriors strove;
> It was beauty that fired their hearts:
> Gwendolen's love.
> On Rhuddlan Marsh <note>original: *Morfa Rhuddlan*</note> plain the rivals stood,
> Until Rhuddlan Marsh plain was drenched in blood:
> Not all proud England's <note>original: *Lloeger*</note> might could Wales <note>original: *Cymry*</note> quell,
> Until foremost of his band, young Griffith fell.

->**3**<-
> Gwendolen saw him fall,
> And "Oh!" the maiden cried;
> "Could a maiden's prayers prevail,
> You would not have died!"
> Distracted, to the plain Gwendolen flew,
> To bathe her hero's wounds—her last adieu!
> Fast over her hero's wounds her tears she shed,
> But tears, alas, are vain—his life had fled.

->**4**<-
> Oh, then for Griffith's son,
> You maidens of Wales <note>original: *Cymry*</note> mourn;
> For well the virgin's tear
> Becomes his funeral urn.
> Nor you, you youths, forbid your tears to flow,
> For those who feel for woe shall best redress it.
> Sweetly sleeps the lovely maid, wept for by the brave,
> For, ah! she died for him she could not save!

---

Rhuddlan Marsh <note>original: *Morfa Rhuddlan*</note>, or the Red Marsh, on the banks of the Clwyd in Flintshire, was the scene of many battles between the Welsh and the Saxons. At the memorable conflict in 795, the Welsh were unsuccessful and their monarch, Caradoc, was slain.

It is unknown whether this celebrated tune took its name from this or some later occasion. The words now adapted to the tune were put into verse <note>original: "verified," likely meaning versified or adapted into poetic form</note> from a fragment published in the *Letters from Snowdon* <note>A collection of letters describing Welsh culture and history, published in 1770</note>. This plaintive style, so predominant in Welsh music, is well-suited to melancholy subjects. Our music probably received a sorrowful quality <note>original: "pathetic tincture"</note> from our hardships under the oppression of the Saxons.

# Old Sibyl <note>original: Hên Sibel</note>

*Brilliant* <note>A musical direction indicating a bright and showy performance style.</note>

> Away; let nothing displeasing to love,
> **my Winifreda**, move your care.
> Let nothing delay the heavenly blessing, neither squeamish pride nor gloomy fear.

2
What though no grants from royal donors
With pompous titles grace our blood!
We will shine with more substantial honors,
And to be noble, we will be good.

3
Our name, while we cherish virtue thus,
Will sweetly sound <note>OCR original: "found"; corrected to "sound" based on 18th-century typography where the long 's' resembles an 'f'.</note> wherever it is spoken:
And all the great people, they shall wonder
How they respect such humble <note>original: "little"</note> folk.

4
What though from Fortune’s lavish bounty,
We possess no mighty treasures,
We will find plenty within our small portion <term>pittance: a small amount or portion of money</term>,
And be content without excess.

5
And each returning season shall still
Give enough for our wishes;
For we will live a life of reason,
And that is the only life to live.

6
Through youth and age, excelling in love,
We will tread hand in hand together;
Sweet-smiling peace shall crown our dwelling,
And babies—sweet-smiling babies—our bed.

7
How I should love the pretty creatures,
While they clung fondly around my knees;
To see them look like their mother,
To hear them speak with their mother’s tongue.

->8<-
->And when time, moved by envy,<-
->Shall think to rob us of our joys;<-
->You will be courted again in your girls,<-
->And I will go wooing in my boys.<-

---

# Ragad, or Reged <note>Reged (Rheged) refers to an ancient Welsh kingdom of the "Old North."</note>

*At a moderate pace* <note>original: Moderato</note>

<note>The beautiful address to married <note>original: conjugal</note> love above is a translation from the Welsh; and I believe it was first printed in a volume of *Miscellaneous Poems*, published by David Lewis in 1726, in octavo <note>original: 8.vo; a book size formed by folding a sheet into eight leaves.</note></note>

<vocab>Hên Sibel, Ragad, Reged, Winifreda, married love, David Lewis, Miscellaneous Poems, Welsh translation, figured bass</vocab>

<summary>This page contains the lyrics to "Old Sibyl" (Hên Sibel), a poem celebrating the simple joys of marriage and family, alongside the instrumental tune "Reged." A historical note attributes the poem to a 1726 collection by David Lewis, claiming it is a translation from Welsh.</summary>
<keywords>Hên Sibel, Old Sibyl, Winifreda, Rheged, David Lewis, Welsh poetry, marriage, domestic virtue, 18th-century music, musical notation</keywords>

<page-num>56</page-num>

# All Through the Night <note>original Welsh: Ar hyd y nos</note>

*Majestic* <note>original: Maestoso</note>

Chorus

## Farewell Ednyfed Fychan <note>original Welsh: Ffarwel Ednyfed Fychan</note>

->**Moderate tempo** <note>original Italian: Moderato</note><-

**EDNYFED FYCHAN, LORD of BRYN FFENIGL**, held great power and authority in Wales during the first part of the 13th century. He was the chief counselor and minister to **LLEWELYN the GREAT**, and the leader of his armies against the **SAXONS**. He usually fought with great success; after bringing back the heads of three Saxon generals whom he had killed with his own hands from one of his battles, he was rewarded by that Prince with a new coat of arms: <term>Gules</term> <note>the heraldic color red</note>, a <term>Chevron</term> <note>a V-shaped heraldic symbol</note> between three <term>Saxons' Heads</term>, <term>proper couped</term> <note>depicted in their natural colors and cut off in a straight line at the neck</note>. From him descended **OWEN TUDOR** of Penmynydd, in the Isle of Anglesey, who married **QUEEN CATHERINE**, the widow of **HENRY V**.

<summary>This page concludes a set of musical variations and provides the historical origins of the air "Ffarwel Ednyfed Fychan," detailing the life and heraldry of the 13th-century Welsh commander Ednyfed Fychan.</summary>
<keywords>Ednyfed Fychan, Llewelyn the Great, Saxon Generals, heraldry, coat of arms, Owen Tudor, Queen Catherine, Welsh history, harp music, variations</keywords>

<page-num>59</page-num>

# David of the White Rock <note>original Welsh: Dafydd y Garreg-wen. David Owen (1711–1741) was a famous Welsh harper and composer.</note>

**Elegiac** <note>A musical direction indicating a mournful, sorrowful, or melancholy style, similar to a funeral song.</note>

There is a widespread tradition in Caernarfonshire that a bard of this name, while lying on his deathbed, called for his harp and performed this mournful tune, which he requested should be repeated at his funeral. Since then, it has been called by his name and that of Carreg-wen, the house where he lived in that county, which still stands. Whether the melody is of greater antiquity, or was originally composed by the dying bard, remains uncertain.

**Languid and Slow**

### The Blackbird’s Stroke <note>original Welsh: Cûr y Fwyalch. This may also be translated as "The Blackbird's Bill" or "The Pecking of the Blackbird."</note>

**In a singing style** <note>original Italian: Cantabile</note>

# The Queen's Dream <note>original Welsh: Breuddwyd y Frenhines</note>

**In the style of an air or song** <note>original Italian: Arioso</note>

---

* <term>SYMLEN BEN BŶS</term> (The Simple One on the Finger-end) was a favorite tune of the great pastoral poet **Dafydd ap Gwilym**, who flourished around the year 1400. He wrote a poem in its praise, in which he informs us that he had learned to play it on his harp. See Rice Jones's *The Masterpieces of the Welsh Bards* <note>original Welsh: Gorchestion Beirdd Cymru</note>, page 18, etc.

# Leaving the Land * <note>original Welsh: Gadael y Tir</note>

**With tender feeling** <note>original Italian: Affettuoso</note>

Of all I have seen beneath the sky,
Of the abundance and bravery of nations,
Of good beer and men to serve it,
And wine on the banks of rivers:
The best beer and the best food 
Were granted to Merioneth <note>original: Meirionydd; a county in North Wales</note>.

---

\* This tune, whose title is **LEAVING THE LAND**, signifies the departure of the Britons from their native country, whether marching to war or emigrating to Ireland or Armorica <note>the ancient name for Brittany, France</note>. The words now set to this music are modern; see page 36.

---

# The Harmonic Ground of Wales
<note>original Welsh: "Cynghan-sail Cymry." "Cynghan" means harmony or consonance, and "sail" means base or foundation. In musical terms, this refers to a "ground bass," a repeating melodic phrase in the bass over which the rest of the music is composed.</note>

---

->The famous Henry Purcell admired this <term>Welsh Ground</term> so much that he imitated it in a <term>Catch</term>.<-
<note>Henry Purcell (1659–1695) was one of England's greatest Baroque composers. A "Catch" is a type of musical round where voices "catch" the melody from one another. A "Welsh Ground" refers to a specific traditional repeating bass pattern common in Welsh harp music.</note>

<summary>This page features the musical notation for "The Harmonic Ground of Wales," a traditional theme followed by eight variations. A historical note explains that the English composer Henry Purcell was a great admirer of this specific Welsh melody.</summary>
